related to budget allocations and disbursements through the reimbursement mechanism, financial statements
and external audits and external audits; and CONAFOR´s regulations on financial management systems. The
GCF grant and loans will be made available to the Government of Mexico under two financing agreements as
described above. IFAD Client Portal will be the online platform used by the Government of Mexico to submit
requests for disbursements to IFAD. Project supervision will be carried out directly by IFAD, independent from
other national control and oversight bodies. From the financial perspective, it will consist of onsite supervisory
missions, which will assess financial management arrangements, identify areas requiring improvement, fiduciary
risks, and corresponding mitigation measures. Financial supervision will also be conducted through office reviews
of periodic financial and annual consolidated audit reports. The Accredited Entity (AE) shall require the Executing
Entity (EE) to report on co-financing by the EE to the satisfaction of the AE. The AE will not be in a position to
validate the data at the time of the submission of the semi-annual or annual reports. The EE’s auditor will certify
the accuracy and reliability of financial information contained. Such audit reports will be submitted by the EE to
the Accredited Entity within 6 months from the end of each the fiscal year. After such 6 months period, any
subsequent disbursement from AE to EE shall be subject to AE having received to its satisfaction the evidence
of the EE co-financing during the preceding fiscal year(s). An interim and final evaluation of the project will be
undertaken aiming at assessing: (i) progress in relation to achievement of results and disbursement performance;
(ii) effectiveness of the institutional and financial arrangements; (iii) the financial procedures manual, suggesting
revisions and updates; (iv) the results of execution and financial management; and (v) achievement of Annual
Work Plans and Budgets (AWPBs). All IFAD reviews, as well as the audits, will be guided by IFAD´s procurement
and eligibility policy and policies to fight fraud and corruption according to Section D (iii) of the IFAD Policy on
Preventing Fraud and Corruption in its Activities and Operation (2018) which regulates anti-corruption
arrangements and responsibilities of IFAD and recipient.
HACIENDA (Executing Entity 1 – “EE1”)
123. HACIENDA is the political and operational government focal point for international climate finance
agencies. It also administers and defines the federal budget and expenditures for implementing public programs,
including those related to climate. The mandate and implementation contract is a legal contract required by the
Mexican legislation by which HACIENDA grants the mandate to a Development Banking Institution (in the case
of Balsas: NAFIN), to act on behalf of the Federal Government as Intermediary between the International
Financial Institutions (in the case of Balsas: IFAD) and the Agencies and Entities executing programs and projects
(in the case of Balsas: CONAFOR) financed with external credit in terms of Preparation, Negotiation; Monitoring
and compliance with contractual commitments; verification of procurement processes; training and technical
assistance, Disbursements of eligible expenses; financial monitoring and debt servicing.
124. Within HACIENDA, the International Financial Affairs Unit (UAIH, by its acronym in Spanish) is in charge
of: i) formulating and undertaking the Public Finance policy in its relations with foreign countries, as well as
negotiations with international financial agencies; ii) exercising the rights, and managing the fulfillment of the
obligations with banks and international organizations; iii) advising on new project proposals linked to external
credit and; iv) negotiating and signing grants and non-reimbursable technical cooperation agreements with
foreign agencies and institutions, as well as coordinating and following up on them.
125. The Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE) is an administrative Unit of the HACIENDA in charge of the
financial management of the Federal Government's resources and securities. In the Balsas project, TESOFE
transfers budgetary resources to CONAFOR, which advances the expenses of the Project activities funded by
the IFAD-GCF Loans. As such, HACIENDA will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded
Activity.
126. As HACIENDA is the authorized Representative of the Borrower, it will sign the Subsidiary Loan
Agreement with IFAD. The UAIH designated NAFIN as the financial agent and CONAFOR as the executing agent
for the GCF Loans. As the proceeds of the GCF Loan A and the GCF Loan B will be disbursed by IFAD to the
Financial Agent (NAFIN), which channels the funds to the Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE) within
HACIENDA, and TESOFE subsequently channels it to CONAFOR, HACIENDA will be an Executing Entity, but it
will not implement any Funded Activity.
NAFIN (Executing Entity 2 – “EE2”)
127. Nacional Financiera, S.N.C., Banca de Desarrollo (NAFIN) is an accredited national Direct Access Entity
(DAE) based in Mexico. As mentioned above, NAFIN will act as the fiduciary of the loans of the Project and will
channel the funds from IFAD to CONAFOR, as CONAFOR cannot directly receive external loan financing. As
such, NAFIN will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity. NAFIN confirms the
disbursement and ensures that the funds have been credited to its account. Subsequently, NAFIN auctions the
funds in the money market, and once the auction is completed, it transfers the equivalent amount in pesos to the

=== PAGE 41 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 37
B
Treasury of the Federation. This includes informing HACIENDA of the value date and the amount in pesos and
dollars of the officially discounted disbursement request. The funds are disbursed in their equivalent in U.S.
dollars, with the TESOFE being notified of the expected receipt date two days prior to the deposit. The proceeds
of GCF Loan A and GCF Loan B, disbursed by IFAD, pass to CONAFOR through the Financial Agent (NAFIN)
and the Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE). NAFIN will be responsible for managing disbursements to IFAD
using the IFAD Client Portal (ICP) already installed at IFAD.
CONAFOR (Executing Entity 3 – “EE3”)
128. The Secretariat of Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT) has been designated to be formally
responsible for implementing the project, and has delegated responsibility for project execution to the National
Forest Commission (CONAFOR), a Decentralized Public Entity of SEMARNAT responsible for implementing the
country's public policy on forestry and with extensive experience in operating projects with international financing.
129. CONAFOR is mandated by the General Law of Sustainable Forest Development to administer the
National Forest Fund (FFM). The National Forest Fund manages all the resources destined for the beneficiaries
of the PADFS (including PES) and other minor CONAFOR programs. CONAFOR will create a sub-account in the
Fondo Forestal Mexicano (FFM) to transparently manage the GCF Grant and GCF Loan resources. The GCF
Loans and GCF grant will have different codes in the SAI system to allow tracking expenditure. Hence, the GCF
Loan and GCF grant will have their respective sub-accounts. A Technical Project Team (ETP) will be created
within CONAFOR, with CONAFOR technical staff who will carry out the comprehensive management of the
technical, administrative, and fiduciary and procurement aspects of project implementation, especially with regard
to the loans administration. CONAFOR has an organic structure and governing board that supervises its
institutional work. Based on its central structure in Zapopan (Jalisco), CONAFOR has an extensive representation
network in the states of Guerrero, Oaxaca and Michoacán. This will ensure the participation of the federal
institutions active in the Balsas Basin as well as its facilitating role of municipal-level participation.
130. CONAFOR has gained extensive experience in executing internationally funded projects by working on
the design and implementation of diverse programs and activities with IFAD, the World Bank, the Forest
Investment Program73, the Bio Carbon Fund, and diverse bilateral cooperation projects managing, and operating
loans of over USD 500 million and grants of approximately USD 25 million. CONAFOR has a 15-year history of
ongoing successful cooperation with IFAD, focused on forest areas and vulnerable populations subject to
exclusion due to their marginalization, poverty, and climate vulnerability. Throughout the formulation and
implementation of these initiatives, CONAFOR has adapted and improved the PADFSB to support sustainable
forest management and ecosystem conservation and restoration by AC, including through Payment of
Environmental Services (PES). CONAFOR has also built its capacity for measurement, reporting and verification
(MRV) and the implementation of Mexico’s REDD+ Strategy. Building on these CONAFOR has consolidated the
FFM, which is a financial instrument to transfer funds to the beneficiaries of the PADFSB allowing for multi-annual
incentive payments for PES and RFM, and the capitalization of public and private financial resources (among
other for the development of Local Mechanisms for PES).
Management, coordination and supervision
The Steering Committee of the Balsas Project (CDAL)
131. At the federal level, a High-Level Steering Committee (Comité Directivo de Alto Nivel or CDAL) will be
created to facilitate inter-institutional coordination of the federal institutions involved in the project implementation.
The CDAL will provide strategic guidance and oversight for project implementation. It will be chaired, hosted, and
convened by CONAFOR, with equal participation of SEMARNAT, CONAFOR, CONAGUA, AGRICULTURA and
CAP. IFAD and HACIENDA will be permanent guests, with voice but without voting rights, while FAO will cover
a technical advisory role when invited to participate. Depending on the needs and issues to be addressed,
additional institutions, representatives from Federal State administrations or other guests may be invited. Within
the CDAL, the co-chairing institutions will be represented at the highest level (National Commissioner, National
Representative, Director General or Executive Director) or by a delegate, if the high-level decision maker is
unable to attend. Decisions of the CDAL will be taken by consensus. It will meet at least twice a year to: (i)
approve the Annual Work Plans and Budget (AWPB); (ii) monitor results, approve quarterly technical and financial
project reports; (iii) follow-up on the co-financing for the project from institution and federal state governments
contributions; (iv) provide for up scaling and replication of project results; (v) provide strategic guidance to the
CONAFOR and co-executing and implementing partners; viii) approve the communication strategy of the Project.
The meetings of the CDAL will be held face to face, virtually, or in a hybrid manner. The CDAL will also be
responsible for the coordination and technical and operational orientation of the Project among the priority

=== PAGE 42 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 38
B
programs of SEMARNAT´s Directorate General for Climate Policy and Action, CONAFOR, CONAGUA and
AGRICULTURA.
The Project Coordination Unit (UCP)
132. The Project Coordination Unit (UCP for its Spanish acronym) will be based in CONAFOR´s headquarters
and will be under the supervision of CONAFOR´s Project Technical Team (ETP). The UCP will prepare the reports
that will be presented by CONAFOR to the CDAL for regular feedback on project implementation. The UCP will
consist of 10 people, of which only 4 will be hired by the Project: the Lead Technical Adviser, one financial and
one procurement specialists, and a Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist (M&E). The main functions of the UCP
will be to ensure efficient project management, coordination, implementation, and monitoring through the effective
execution of the AWPB, support and supervision of field personnel and experts, quality assurance and safeguard
compliance. The UCP will be also contributing to the financial management of GCF and IFAD financial resources
in accordance with CONAFOR´s and IFAD´s regulations and procedures. The UCP will be in charge of (i)
integrating the AWPB to be presented to the CONAFOR and CDAL, based on the investment plans developed
in prioritized sub-basin and territories; (ii) follow-up on support to RCIMTs; (iii) contributing to the capacity
development of CONAFOR´s Promotorías and extension workers; (iv.) facilitating the access to technical
assistance to the communities and ensuring the technical supervision of all project activities; (v) facilitating the
contribution of CONAFOR´s different Technical Divisions, including GreenMex´s project team with project
activities in the prioritized project areas, and the alignment of activities with the PADFSB; (vi) preparing the
quarterly technical and financial reports; (vii) monitoring project indicators in accordance with the logical
framework; (viii) ensuring the development of the project baseline, midterm review, and final evaluation; ix) ensure
close collaboration between the project activities and other relevant partners at the national, state and municipality
levels; and (x) preparation of Terms of Reference (ToR) for the recruitment of consultants and firms, which will
be funded by GCF grant resources.
The Regional Committees for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCIMT)
133. RCIMTs are multi-sectoral participation and consultation entities at Forest Management Unit (FMU) level.
They will provide advice to CONAFOR and allow for the participation of the local stakeholders in the formulation
of local strategies, prioritization of the most vulnerable areas, and monitoring of progress. Moreover, the RCMITs
will support a better coordination of interventions between institutions at the federal, state, and municipality levels.
The committee´s representatives will be selected by state governments, municipalities, agrarian communities,
indigenous communities, organizations of farmers, private companies and other relevant sectoral representatives
and actors. They will meet at least once each quarter to: (i) discuss on the Project intervention strategy in the
FMU, (ii) approve the prioritization of micro basins; (iii) identify needed financial and technical support and the
federal or state programs to cover their demand; and (iv) orient action plans to ensure that the different
stakeholders coordinate their intervention and mobilize resources; among other duties. The Local Promotoría of
CONAFOR will be responsible for the announcement, preparation of the agenda and elaboration of the minutes,
as well as ensuring that the members of the RCIMT will have access to the needed information regarding the
project design, implementation procedures and progress reports.
CONAFOR´s Forestry Development Offices (Promotorías forestales)
134. CONAFOR operates Forestry Development Offices called “Promotorías Forestales” at the state
(regional) and local levels to support the implementation of the PADFSB. At the state level, the main functions of
the promotorías are the promotion of the PADFSB, identification of priorities in the state; development of
coordination mechanisms with the State administration and especially with its environment-related entities; and
participation in the selection of the applications for PADFSB incentives submitted by the agrarian communities
and other forestry organizations. Within the Balsas Basin, there are seven State level promotorías but only those
of Oaxaca, Guerrero, Puebla and Michoacán cover the 10 prioritized project intervention areas. At the local level,
the promotorías forestales are located close to the agrarian communities where the PADFSB can be potentially
implemented. In each of these small entities, there is a team of forestry facilitators recruited by CONAFOR, whose
functions consist of disseminating information on the PADFSB and other initiatives of the environmental sector.
They are also responsible for providing technical support to the agrarian communities in their efforts of planning
and implementing sustainable forest management, restoration of the ecosystems, the provision of environmental
services, and the development of agrarian and productive structures. The location of the existing local
promotorías in the Balsas basin will allow allocating one for each sub-basin (see map below). The team of the
promotorías forestales will assist the Project UCP lead adviser in facilitating RCIMT meetings to improve the
coordination with other institutions and programs operating in the Sub-Basin. The Project will strengthen the
promotorías through the recruitment of additional 26 forestry facilitators, and the acquisition of equipment.
Technical assistance and training will be also provided by CONAFOR´s team and experts recruited by the Project,

=== PAGE 43 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 39
B
especially about adaptive forestry, utilization of different information systems, implementation of safeguard,
gender, and social inclusion.

Area
Source: CONAFOR (2022)
Project coordination with other relevant institutions, programs, projects, and initiatives
135. One of the most important public policy challenges in rural Mexico is achieving greater efficiency in terms
of quality and outcomes through the various priority programs concentrating the largest shares of government
spending in rural areas. This raises the need for maximum synergy, inter-agency coordination and fostering
converging actions across federal entities and territories.
136. SEMARNAT aims to incorporate in the different spheres of the Mexican society and the public sector
criteria and instruments that ensure protection, conservation, and sustainable use of the country's natural
resources, thus forming an integral and inclusive environmental policy that will allow to achieve sustainable
development. In order to comply with the above, SEMARNAT, its three sub-secretariats and the various
deconcentrated and decentralized entities, such as CONAFOR and CONAGUA, work on four priority aspects: i)
The conservation and sustainable use of ecosystems and their biodiversity, ii) Pollution prevention and control,
iii) Integral management of water resources.; and iv) Combating climate change. As leader of the Environment
Sector, SEMARNAT provides advice and supervises CONAFOR and CONAGUA participation in the Balsas PA,
and revises the project budget to be submitted to HACIENDA. Especially, the Directorate General for Climate
Policy and Action of SEMARNAT, which is the entity in charge of the National Institute of Ecology and Climate
Change (INECC)74, will support CONAFOR in assessing climate change and perception, diagnosis and
vulnerability in the prioritized areas of the Balsas PA, contribute to adaptation measures and monitoring in the
intervened areas, train different actors of the project on climate change issues, provide climate change scenarios
and support in the calculation of GHG emissions and participate in different technical working groups, including
the RCIMT´s.
137. CONAGUA undertakes functions related to the national water policy and regulation for its use
(concessions, permits, and sanctions for non-compliance), as well as technical and financial support to the states

=== PAGE 44 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 40
B
and municipalities in the development of urban and rural hydraulic infrastructures. The Entity is also in charge of
the National Meteorological Service (SMN) and collaborates in civil protection programs, addressing emergencies
due to extreme weather events. Programs such as PROAGUA related to better management of water in the
prioritized municipalities offers a significant opportunity for collaboration and co-financing. Within the project
intervention area, CONAGUA operates two main Administrative Offices (Regions RHA IV; RHA V), each with its
own units that contribute to the implementation of PROAGUA and other programs. The communities that do not
have access to tap water or suffer from irregular provision will be identified in each municipality. Through the
support of the RCIMTs, the Project team will seek synergy with the State administrations, municipalities, and the
PROAGUA program to facilitate the necessary co-funding. The investment will consist of rainwater catchment for
households, management of micro basins to build small hydraulic infrastructure and development of irrigation
systems.
138. AGRICULTURA The Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development leads the formulation,
implementation, and evaluation of the general rural development policy. Among other activities, AGRICULTURA
operates programs benefiting small farmers, through the transfer of subsidies and provision of technical
assistance. At sub regional level, AGRICULTURA operates through the Rural Center for Rural Development
(CADER). As co-implementing partners of the project, AGRICULTURA will support the following activities: i)
identification of the interventions in the prioritized areas that are aligned with the project, ii) sharing of information
on agricultural production, soils, the effects of climate change on agriculture, and productive re-conversion
ongoing efforts; iii) technical assistance to the prioritized communities to strengthen capacities especially in
agroforestry, silvo-pastoral systems and traspatio production; iv) coordination with CONAFOR to improve fire
management and protection, and vi) facilitating collaboration with the sub-regional structures (CADER) and
Research Centers that can provide technical assistance and financial support to the producers.
139. State and municipality Administrations in CONSEJOS FORESTALES ESTATALES (CFE). It is strategic
for the Balsas Project to involve the State and municipal governments, especially in the prioritized sub-basin,
mainly located in the states of Guerrero, Oaxaca, Michoacán, and Puebla. In that sense, the CFE promoted by
CONAFOR, and the State administration are relevant entities to share information and seek synergies. The CFE,
in which state authorities, forestry organizations and municipalities are represented, will serve to establish specific
collaboration agreements and co-investments, facilitating, in the prioritized municipalities, the possibility for states
and municipal governments to participate financially in the project initiatives, such as the Local Mechanisms for
Payment for Environment Services, micro basin restoration, or the implementation of PROAGUA. In the recently
elected state administrations of Oaxaca, Guerrero and Michoacán, the Project will promote the inclusion of the
Balsas project objectives within the State Development Plans.
140. Engagement of the private sector The project will seek to create partnerships with business organizations
and private companies, with the aim of both developing innovative schemes for the payment of environmental
services and the integration of inclusive value chains. Private sector engagement could be oriented to:
• Create Local Mechanisms for Payment for Environment Services, based on the experience and
instruments developed by CONAFOR, with the purpose of promoting the participation of private
companies that use environmental services (water, landscape, carbon sinks, etc.).
• Foster the financial inclusion of forestry and other social enterprises with Multiple Purpose Financial
Companies (SOFOMs in Spanish) that have already opened credit lines to finance green and inclusive
business models.
• Support access to the Guarantees Funds that CONAFOR created to facilitate the access of credit to
community forestry companies for the development of partnerships with private companies for timber and
non-timber forest value chains . Emphasis will be given on facilitating access to credits for small-scale
producers, especially women7,5 young people, and indigenous producers to develop commercial
agreements with the private sector in conditions of fairness and equity.
• Specific needs of industrial processes that allow the inclusion of women and youth in the forestry and
agroforestry value chains, tapping into potential green and fair-trade markets. Products with a high
potential for such agri-business partnerships are pine and other tree resin, timber, coffee, agave, and
palm fibres.
Within this diverse and challenging institutional setting, CONAFOR and its partners have negotiated and agreed
on roles and functions for its project partners and project entities thar are systematized in the following matrix:

141. Fund flow for the GCF Loans – red arrows (Figure 14):
• GCF transfers GCF Loan A and GCF Loan B resources to IFAD in accordance with signed financial
agreement between both entities.
• IFAD channels (or “reimburses”) eligible funds to a bank account managed by NAFIN.
• NAFIN channels the resources received to the TESOFE.
• The Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE) managed by HACIENDA channels budgetary resources to
CONAFOR (and in particular the Fondo Forestal Mexicano (FFM)), which pays for the expenses of the
Project activities funded by the IFAD-GCF Loans.
• (a) CONAFOR spends the budgetary funds of the Project and processes the payments destined for
goods and works, consultancy services, non-consultancy services, training, and incremental costs of the
project; (b) CONAFOR selects the beneficiaries, signs the agreements to deliver the financial incentives
to the eligible beneficiaries, and instructs BANORTE, financial agent of the FFM, to process the
payments.
• The beneficiaries send reports and information to CONAFOR, in accordance with the provisions of the
PADFSB Operating Rules and the agreement signed between the parties.
• Based on the reports submitted by the beneficiaries and the payments processed to consultants and
suppliers, CONAFOR aggregates and consolidates financial information in the Statements of
Expenditures (SOE), and formally sends them to NAFIN.
• NAFIN reviews the Statements of Expenditures and sends them to IFAD, together with the next
disbursement requests for the GCF Loans and grant.
• IFAD reviews and verifies those Statements of Expenditures and prepares the Annual Performance
Reports and semi-annual financial reports in line with the AMA and FAA.
• .
• Fund flow for the Grant (see Figure 14):
• GCF transfers grant resources to IFAD in accordance with signed financial agreement between both
entities.

=== PAGE 51 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 47
B
• IFAD channels resources to CONAFOR (and in particular the Fondo Forestal Mexicano (FFM) , in
accordance with the total amount of the activities planned in the Annual Operating Plan presented by this
entity.
• (a) CONAFOR spends the grant and processes the payments destined for direct supports, goods and
works, consultancy services, non-consultancy services, training, and incremental costs of the project; (b)
In the case of direct supports, CONAFOR selects the beneficiaries and the supported ACs’ initiatives,
according to the approved operational guidelines, signs the agreements to deliver the financial incentives
to the eligible beneficiaries, and instructs BANORTE, financial agent of the FFM, to process the payments
• The beneficiaries send reports and information to CONAFOR, in accordance with the provisions of the
special guidelines that the Project prepare and the agreement signed between the parties.
• Based on the reports submitted by the beneficiaries and the payments processed to consultants and
suppliers, CONAFOR aggregates and consolidates financial information in the Statements of
Expenditures (SOE), sending the proofs of the eligible expenses to accredit the expenses made. The
SOE is reported to IFAD.
• IFAD reviews and verifies those Statements of Expenditures and prepares the Annual Performance
Reports and semi-annual financial reports in line with the AMA and FAA.
No transfers of funds will be made to any other participating institutions identified in the Funding proposal.
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
142. Justification The project GCF financing is made up of GCF Loans worth USD 20 million, which consist of
a USD 10 million loan with high concessionality (GCF Loan A) and a USD 10 million loan with low concessionality
(GCF Loan B), and a USD 19.5 million grant that, together with an IFAD loan of USD 38.453 million aim at
consolidating and scaling up innovative forest development financing lines for climate change adaptation and
mitigation under CONAFOR´s PADFSB and the programs of the other agencies involved. It also promotes a
scheme of inter-institutional convergence of priority budgetary actions and programs of the Government of Mexico
in the Balsas PA with lines of environmental financing and climate change adaptation and mitigation, coordinating
actions and maximizing impacts. The investment loans from external sources will make it possible for CONAFOR
to maintain the minimum investment needs of the PADFSB in the PA in a context of fiscal restraint.
143. The project contemplates a national counterpart of USD 13 million that mainly includes CONAFOR's
operating expenses, funding for land use change compensation (CA_CUS) and CONAGUA's investments from
its PROAGUA program. The GCF grant will be essential to provide the necessary technical assistance to promote
the paradigm shift, inter-institutional convergence and to generate innovative productive models for adaptation to
climate change, as also to finance productive models that are not financed within the PADFSB (such as MIAF
systems or support for backyard agriculture), and the financing of forest monitoring brigades of young people and
women. In contrast to the loan resources, under Mexican regulations, the grant will secure financial resources
additional to those allocated to CONAFOR by the Ministry of Finance from the fiscal budget.
144. The Balsas Project targets the poorest and most vulnerable rural communities in the Balsas basin,
including indigenous peoples, women, and youth. Of the highly prioritized municipalities, there are 121
municipalities of the Project which have very high and high vulnerability and very high and high poverty levels
and include 17 municipalities located in two indigenous territories.
145. The poorest and most vulnerable rural communities of those prioritized municipalities have very limited
capital available and lack access to finance, making them dependent on short-term returns to meet their
immediate needs. Investing in forestry and/or agroforestry will not yield any positive financial returns in the
first 3-5 years due to the significant upfront costs and the time it takes for trees to reach maturity and generate
revenue (negative cashflows in first years). Hence, the investments and activities related to forestry and
agroforestry within the BALSAS project would not occur without the required level of concessional support
from the GCF (in particular the grant, but also GCF Loan A). This is also why HACIENDA on behalf of the
Government of Mexico will be taking on GCF Loan A and GCF Loan B and will be reimbursing the GCF Loans
to the GCF via IFAD. The beneficiaries, in turn, will receive the project financing from the Government of
Mexico in the form of non-reimbursable grants.
146. Importantly, it is worth noting that in the absence of concessional support from the GCF, the paradigm
shift is unlikely to occur. The current government programs would continue to be implemented in their current
form and would neither reach the highly vulnerable communities identified by the GCF Balsas project, nor

would they allow for climate change criteria to be included for innovative production models for adaptation to
climate change to be financed within the PADFSB.
147. IFAD and the Government of Mexico carefully costed the project in a way that concessional resources
(GCF Grant and GCF Loan A) will be non-revenue generating, while the least concessional resources (Loan
B) will be used for revenue-generating activities.
- USD 19.5 million GCF grant resources will be used to finance exclusively non-revenue generating
activities and are either:
tailored to cover specific activities where the benefits and repayment of the investment are less
o
evident and would therefore only be viable with high levels of concessionality, such as
institutional strengthening activities, technical assistance, monitoring and evaluation and project
management (Activities 1.1.1., 1.1.2, 1.1.3, 1.2.1, 1.2.2, 1.3.1., 1.3.2, 1.3.3., 2.3.3., 3.1.1, 3.1.2,
3.1.3, 3.1.4, 3.2.2, 3.2.3), through e.g. Activity 1.3.1: Capacity development programs for
extension workers, local facilitators, and forest protection “brigades”, which essentially is TA for
agroforestry and afforestation.
tailored to incremental costs in the case of Activity 2.3.2, where the grant serves the crucial
o
purpose of providing necessary injections to kickstart the market for an activity which would
otherwise not occur as it is financially not viable (financial internal rate of return of 0.19% is less
than the financial discount rate of 6%). In the absence of the GCF grant, the diversified milpa
systems with agroforestry wouldn’t be established as they are currently not part of PADFS
support and there are no alternative sources of finances. In the hypothetical case of access to
credit, high interest rates would hinder any positive scenario on return on investment. Besides,
the vulnerable communities don't have the means to repay loans.
- USD 20 million of GCF Loans consist of a USD 10 million GCF Loan with high concessionality (Loan A)
and a GCF Loan with low concessionality (GCF Loan B).
USD 10 million GCF Loan A resources will be used to finance a PES scheme to reach the
o
prioritized poor and vulnerable communities in the Balsas basin (Activity 2.1.1). A highly
concessional loan for the Balsas PES scheme (Activity 2.1.1) is justified as it is non-revenue
generating and avoids potentially higher costs of addressing deforestation-related problems in
the future. Compared to other sectors (e.g. energy), Forest Lands and ecosystems usually
receive a much higher proportion of grant finance (e.g. RIOS project in Mexico with 100% grant).
Using a GCF Loan for a PES scheme is a key innovation that fosters improved incentives for
scaling up forest establishment and maintenance activities as it brings co-responsibility for
natural resources conservation.
USD 10 million GCF Loan B resources will be used to finance all revenue-generating activities
o
of the project (activities for which the financial internal rate of return exceed the discount rate of
6%), i.e. Activities 1.3.2, 2.2.1 and 2.2.2. Activity 1.3.2 (Forest Protection Programs) in Outcome
1 has a mix of grant and loan financing, with grants allocated to the non-revenue generating part
of this activity (Local consultants and travel to support positive action measures to incorporate
women, youth and indigenous peoples). The loan resources will be allocated to public
expenditures cost-saving activities (as it will reduce the probability of outbreaks involving forest
fires & pests or diseases for agricultural practices, that would require the government to provide
emergency response).
148. Concessionality Transfers of project resources to beneficiaries are carried out under strict targeting
criteria to benefit the poorest and most vulnerable populations. These beneficiaries face steep barriers to credit
access. Although CONAFOR manages two funds for forestry project loans (FONAFOR and FOSEFOR), none of
them has been able to place their funds. This is due to the long project-cycle of the forest sector characterized
by a long time (in years) from the investment until the first benefits occur, and even longer until the investment
has a financial return. Such long periods are impossible to bear for the targeted population, if financial incentives
are not provided. Second, most of these beneficiaries lack a collateral, which excludes them from most if not all
existing credit sources, including CONAFOR´s credit funds. In this sense, the grant resources are required to
make this investment proposal viable.
149. In accordance with national regulations, external credit financial resources (loans) must be included
within the yearly budget allocated to an institution. This implies that CONAFOR must include within its annual
budget ceiling the necessary resources to execute the external credit loans it is responsible for. In terms of their
execution, in accordance with national policy, the loans are implemented through the incentives granted by
CONAFOR through its incentive program PADFSB. Loan resources cannot be used to finance operating
expenses or incremental costs associated with the project. In contrast, as CONAFOR is a decentralized public
body of the Federal Public Administration, with its own legal personality and assets, grants from international
funding are additional to its regular budget. Non-reimbursable resources represent funding for the operation and
implementation of the project. Therefore, grant resources play a key role in the successful implementation of the

 | 6%), i.e. Activities 1.3.2, 2.2.1 and 2.2.2. Activity 1.3.2 (Forest Protection Programs) in Outcome | 
 | 1 has a mix of grant and loan financing, with grants allocated to the non-revenue generating part | 
 | of this activity (Local consultants and travel to support positive action measures to incorporate | 
 | women, youth and indigenous peoples). The loan resources will be allocated to public | 
 | expenditures cost-saving activities (as it will reduce the probability of outbreaks involving forest | 
 | fires & pests or diseases for agricultural practices, that would require the government to provide | 
 | emergency response). | 
 | 148. Concessionality Transfers of project resources to beneficiaries are carried out under strict targeting | 
 | criteria to benefit the poorest and most vulnerable populations. These beneficiaries face steep barriers to credit | 
 | access. Although CONAFOR manages two funds for forestry project loans (FONAFOR and FOSEFOR), none of | 
 | them has been able to place their funds. This is due to the long project-cycle of the forest sector characterized | 
 | by a long time (in years) from the investment until the first benefits occur, and even longer until the investment | 
 | has a financial return. Such long periods are impossible to bear for the targeted population, if financial incentives | 
 | are not provided. Second, most of these beneficiaries lack a collateral, which excludes them from most if not all | 
 | existing credit sources, including CONAFOR´s credit funds. In this sense, the grant resources are required to | 
 | make this investment proposal viable. | 
 | 149. In accordance with national regulations, external credit financial resources (loans) must be included | 
 | within the yearly budget allocated to an institution. This implies that CONAFOR must include within its annual | 
 | budget ceiling the necessary resources to execute the external credit loans it is responsible for. In terms of their | 
 | execution, in accordance with national policy, the loans are implemented through the incentives granted by | 
 | CONAFOR through its incentive program PADFSB. Loan resources cannot be used to finance operating | 
 | expenses or incremental costs associated with the project. In contrast, as CONAFOR is a decentralized public | 
 | body of the Federal Public Administration, with its own legal personality and assets, grants from international | 
 | funding are additional to its regular budget. Non-reimbursable resources represent funding for the operation and | 
 | implementation of the project. Therefore, grant resources play a key role in the successful implementation of the | 

=== PAGE 53 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49
B
project, as they allow to pay for specialized technical assistance, innovative studies and external support that are
not covered in the regular operations. It also allows for the financing of capacity building for both beneficiaries
and institutions that facilitate the implementation and achievement of the project objective. In view of the above,
as well as in view of COVID-19 related fiscal limitations, grant resources as an addition to the current budget will
be essential for the project to be implemented appropriately and effectively.
150. Additionality GCF funds will fill the existing climate finance gap to contribute to meeting the Nationally
Determined Contributions (NDCs) in the Balsas PA. To meet fiscal targets the government budget linked to the
environmental sector has been lowered by 32% from years 2016 to 2019, which puts at risk the fulfilment of the
Paris Agreement targets on climate change76. Figure 15 below shows that between years 2015 and 2020 the
budget for the environmental sector has been halved, with sensitive reductions to the budgets of the CONAFOR
(70%) and CONAGUA (60%). At constant prices, in the year 2019 Mexico´s investments for environment and
climate change was as high as during the year 2001. This situation is being exacerbated by the COVID-19
pandemic, as public resources are reallocated to emergency response and care, altering medium-term priorities
and targets for climate finance. GCF funds will leverage public funding and funding from other agencies, including
IFAD, the GEF and others, to meet the targets set.
Figure 15: Federal budget assigned to the institutions of the environmental sector in Mexico (in millions MXN)
Source: El Economista, México (09/2019)77
151. According to the database on the budgetary situation of HACIENDA78, between 2019 and 2020,
budgetary revenues decreased by 1.3% and remained stagnated between 2020 and 2021 (0.1% increase) while
primary expenditure shows a constant increase from 2019 to 2022 (0.1% in 2020, 6.3% in 2021 and 5.1% in
2022). Primary balance resulted in expenditures exceeding revenues for 0.3% of GDP in 2021. This allows us to
anticipate limited availability of public funding for this type of investment due to the fiscal situation and the priorities
of emergency attention in response to the consequences and recovery of COVID19.
152. The project combines sources of finance to transform production systems and land use into production
systems that are more adapted to climate change in one of the poorest regions of the country, which is among
the most vulnerable to climate change and variability nationwide. This raises the perceived risk for the private
sector to invest. Through GCF, IFAD and GEF funding, joint and coordinated actions for better governance,
mitigation and adaptation to climate change will be implemented, which will reduce the identified risk, leverage
more public and private investment, and scale up existing best practices and business models. The
concessionality with which resources are transferred to beneficiaries will be determined by their level of
vulnerability. Grant resources are required to enable specific investments perceived as higher risk by beneficiaries
and the government, as well as to cover a portion of incremental project operating costs.
The project has a high potential for replicability in other regions of the country or can even be a model that can
be applied to other Latin American countries that may present similar conditions. CONAFOR will be able to
replicate the models, practices and criteria generated by this project for its application to the PADFSB at the
national level and for cross-fertilization with similar public forest landscape restoration programs within South-
South cooperation projects.

B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
153. The project's sustainability and replicability depend on transforming institutional modalities (the
Operational Rules of the PADFSB) used to allocate traditional forest development incentives, re-aligning them as
climate finance for adaptation and mitigation initiatives. While the PADFSB is a national program, the approach
will be piloted in the most vulnerable areas of the Balsas PA. As described in Section B.2 above and D.2 further
below, the project’s financial exit strategy is built into this paradigm-shift. Through this approach an average of
USD 160 million per year79 of traditional forestry incentives at the national level is expected to be re-aligned
towards integral, climate adapted territorial development finance which adds-up to more than USD 1.5 billion
nationwide during the project lifespan of 20 years (please see Figure 16 below). The piloting of this approach by
CONAFOR through intensive capacity building and technical assistance in the Balsas Basis provides a model
that shall be replicated and scaled-up across Mexico’s climate-vulnerable areas without further external financial
contributions.
154. The proposed model builds on the existing experiences and relationships which exist between IFAD,
CONAFOR and the Government of Mexico. It also integrates lessons learned from the previous projects in terms
of project design and implementation. The project will seek to strengthen institutional and multi-sectoral
coordination at the state and basin level. It also aims to incorporate a climate approach into the design and
implementation of government priority programs which are currently contributing to the NDCs commitments. The
medium and long-term sustainability of the project is also based on a Memorandum of Understanding to be signed
between IFAD and CONAFOR for the medium and long term sustainability of the project after its natural End of
8 years with the commitment to further:
● Establishing participatory mechanisms for local and micro-regional planning and setting up social rules that
support territorial land-use management while taking into account the threats and opportunities linked to
climate change.
● Developing forestry, agroforestry and adaptive forestry system technologies which promote native tree
species better adapted to climate change.
● Encouraging existing rural extension systems to incorporate a climate dimension into their technical
assistance services and strengthening organizational and technology transfers, especially for the most
vulnerable populations.
155. The project has strong potential for replicability in other regions of the country. It may even serve as an
applicable model in other Latin American countries or areas with similar conditions to the project area. CONAFOR
may further develop the models and practices emerging from this project to apply them at the national level.

--- Table 1 on Page 54 ---

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
157. This cross-cutting project will be contributing to the goals of Mex’co's NDCs in terms of mitigation by reducing
emissions linked to the agriculture, forestry, and other land use sector (AFOLU). But the main contribution will be in
terms of adaptation under the NDC’s: axis–A - preventing and responding to the negative impacts on the territory and
human population; axis–B - resilient production systems and food security; axis–C - conservation, rehabilitation and
the sustainable use of biodiversity and ecosystem services; and axis–D - integrated water resource management with
a climate change approach. The mitigation and adaptation lines of ac–ion in the NDCs for the AFOLU sector are fully
integrated and aligned with each other. The project’s specific contributions and alignment with the NDCs can be
found in Annex 2 (Feasibility Study).
158. The proposed project, although cross-cutting, is primarily focused on adaptation. The project will foster
climate change adaptation impacts in the 10 prioritized areas of the Balsas PA in four ways:
● Basin ecosystems will generate environmental services, strengthening the local population’s capacity for
adaptation
● Groups most vulnerable to climate change will increase their income, food security and access to water
● Climate risks affecting the population and their productive systems will be reduced; and
● Dry season water supplies will be supplemented through investments in small-scale rainwater harvesting and
water supply infrastructure.
Adaptation
159. The project beneficiaries will be primarily vulnerable rural small producers and households living in poverty
and indigenous peoples present in the intervention area including: (i) agrarian communities (ACs) mainly owned by
indigenous peoples; (ii) farmer organizations within the ACs (cooperatives and other formal and informal figures); (iii)

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA |  | 
 | This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the | 
 | GCF’s Initial Investment Framework. | 
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) |  | 
 | 157. This cross-cutting project will be contributing to the goals of Mex’co's NDCs in terms of mitigation by reducing | 
 | emissions linked to the agriculture, forestry, and other land use sector (AFOLU). But the main contribution will be in | 
 | terms of adaptation under the NDC’s: axis–A - preventing and responding to the negative impacts on the territory and | 
 | human population; axis–B - resilient production systems and food security; axis–C - conservation, rehabilitation and | 
 | the sustainable use of biodiversity and ecosystem services; and axis–D - integrated water resource management with | 
 | a climate change approach. The mitigation and adaptation lines of ac–ion in the NDCs for the AFOLU sector are fully | 
 | integrated and aligned with each other. The project’s specific contributions and alignment with the NDCs can be | 
 | found in Annex 2 (Feasibility Study). | 
 | 158. The proposed project, although cross-cutting, is primarily focused on adaptation. The project will foster | 
 | climate change adaptation impacts in the 10 prioritized areas of the Balsas PA in four ways: | 
 | ● Basin ecosystems will generate environmental services, strengthening the local population’s capacity for | 
 | adaptation | 
 | ● Groups most vulnerable to climate change will increase their income, food security and access to water | 
 | ● Climate risks affecting the population and their productive systems will be reduced; and | 
 | ● Dry season water supplies will be supplemented through investments in small-scale rainwater harvesting and | 
 | water supply infrastructure. | 
 |  | 
 | Adaptation | 
 | 159. The project beneficiaries will be primarily vulnerable rural small producers and households living in poverty | 
 | and indigenous peoples present in the intervention area including: (i) agrarian communities (ACs) mainly owned by | 
 | indigenous peoples; (ii) farmer organizations within the ACs (cooperatives and other formal and informal figures); (iii) | 

=== PAGE 58 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 54
D
women and youth with limited access to productive assets and other vulnerable groups such as Afro-Mexican
population; and (iv) some small private owners interested in participating in forestry and agroforestry development
programs. Direct beneficiaries of the project are defined as small producers of ACs from 121 of the most social and
climate vulnerable municipalities in the Balsas PA, focusing on indigenous communities (82 of the 121 municipalities
are indigenous). Indirect beneficiaries of the project are defined as members of the households of the direct
beneficiaries, as well as other community members from the 68 ACs targeted. The estimated number of direct project
beneficiaries is 201 867 people, and the project’s indirect beneficiaries are estimated at 3 107 344 which represent
9.25% of the population of the Balsas PA. Women and youth participation is estimated at 40% and 30% respectively,
with indigenous people representing 60% of total beneficiaries. The project will also include other vulnerable groups,
mainly the Afro-Mexican population. Moreover, in order ensure greater participation and the social inclusion of the
project’s main target groups, a combination of direct targeting and self-targeting methods will be put in place.
Mitigation
160. While primarily adaptation focused (80%), the cross-cutting project also includes a considerable mitigation
component (20%). The project will contribute to the 2030 national target of reducing GHG emissions by 22%. This
emission-reduction target will be achieved by implementing the following activities: (i) in the land use, land use
change and forestry sector: afforestation, reforestation and forest, farmland, and pasture management; and (ii) in the
agricultural sector: agricultural soil management activities and programmed soil burns. CONAFOR has determined
that project activities may contribute to mitigating 3 926 876 MtCO over the 20-year period. This supports the
2eq
strong potential which may exist for reducing emissions by slowing deforestation, increasing forest-related carbon
reservoirs, and implementing low-carbon technologies. These actions will lead to low-carbon development models
based on sustainable forestry and agricultural activities. Mitigation calculations are presented in Annex 22.
161. The Balsas Project will contribute to the achievement of all of the seven milestones set by the ENAREDD+
for the year 2030 (se also paragraph 35 above). Furthermore, the Project supports the implementation of a l the seven
components and most of its action lines and activities of the ENAREDD+, as shown in the table included in Annex 2a.
It is important to note that the ENAREDD+ sees new models addressing the causes of deforestation and forest
degradation as pivotal to reaching zero deforestation by 2030, a goal also specified in the NDCs. The project
responds to this requirement. It also outlines a path that will help the Government of Mexico develop these necessary
new models. These models will help address existing challenges by applying a cross-cutting watershed management
approach to mitigation and adaptation. They may also generate a mechanism for distributing resulting benefits to the
most vulnerable population.
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
162. The core medium- to long-term result of the project to adjust the Rules of Operation of the PADFSB to
include and operationalize climate change criteria for territorial development finance lead to the paradigm shift, first
catalyzed through intense technical assistance and capacity building in the pilot Balsas PA using GCF concessional
finance, that will impact also at the national level. The annual expected average of USD 160 million per year of
traditional forestry finance at the national level sums up to the total amount of USD 1.54 billion (net present value)
during the project lifespan (20 years) of re-aligned traditional forestry incentives, towards climate finance. Additionally,
there is great potential for this leveraged finance to be increased further through the application of the same paradigm
shifting to other rural development finance sources in Mexico at the federal level, such as CONAGUA´s and
AGRICULTURA´s programs, through the strengthening of multisectoral coordination structures that will be reinforced
by the project.
163. The average of USD 160 million per year that the project aims to realign has been calculated from two
CONAFOR programmes: Support for Sustainable Forest Development (S219) and Forest Protection (E014). These
initiatives were allocated on average USD 94,805,800 (S219) and USD 67,330,400 (E014) per annum over the years
2017-2021, which represents a combined national average of USD 164,740,565 per year between the two initiatives
(see Table 7 below). Given the consistency of the average annual budget allocations across these two initiatives over
these 5 years, it is assumed that at least USD 160 million has the potential to be reassigned as climate finance per
year via the project’s paradigm-shifting (a financial analysis depicting this is presented in Figure 16 below). The
annual average of USD 160 million sums up to the total amount of USD 1.5 billion in 20 years of re-aligned traditional
forestry incentives, towards climate finance. This value was derived as net present value using a conservative social
discount rate of 10% (as defined by the Government of Mexico in 2022 — see Annex 10). The sensitivity analysis of
the re-aligned climate finance during the project’s lifespan to different discount rates is also presented in Table 8
below.

=== PAGE 60 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 56
D
164. A continued flow of resources towards USD 1.5 billion in re-aligned, leveraged climate finance is expected to
be guaranteed by the resources budgeted by the Government of Mexico for integral territorial development over the
next 20 years. The origin of this territorial integral development finance, disbursed by CONAFOR, is the Fondo
Forestal Mexicano (FFM), which manages all the resources destined for the beneficiaries of the PADFSB (including
PES) and other minor CONAFOR programs. Approximately 48% of this territorial integral forest development finance
includes resources from various national sectors that pay the Government of Mexico a compensation fee related to
land use change caused by development in the respective sectors. The remaining 51% is from government fiscal
resources, including a contribution from CONAGUA transfers (~USD 30 million/year) deriving from the management
of dams. Given the origin and the continuity during the last decade of the integral forest development funds described
above, there is a high potential for the estimated total of USD 1.5 billion of re-aligned climate finance at national level
to be achieved, or even surpassed, because of the very conservative discount rate chose for calculating its net
present value.
Project design aspects that contribute to the paradigm shift
165. The project will transform production systems and land use in the Balsas PA region, targeting the most
climate vulnerable and poor rural communities in the country, specifically the most vulnerable municipalities with the
highest levels of poverty and extreme poverty. This cross-cutting project (80% adaptation, 20% mitigation) aims to
increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and reduce emissions from
production systems in the Basin, via: i) operationalizing the use of climate change criteria in current institutional

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 57
D
modalities for the prioritization of rural development support in the Balsas PA through a community-centric bottom-up
approach; ii) investing in the climate-sensitive management of ecosystems, and climate-resilient productive systems
and value chains for adaptation and mitigation benefits; and iii) developing a climate information system, and
implementation of knowledge management. The proposed project will shift the approach of the current forestry
development support allocation system used by rural development institutions to one which:
● Uses climate change-related criteria for selecting and prioritizing the most vulnerable beneficiaries,
interventions and sites.
● Strengthen local watershed governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the
sub-basin level to grant incentives and for interinstitutional coordination and a bottom-up approach for the
sustainable use management and conservation of crucial ecosystems and ecosystem services to be piloted
in the Balsas PA and replicated at national level. As part of the project’s bottom-up approach, the
participation of Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations, especially women and youth, in value
chains of timber and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) will also be strengthened, with the aim of increasing
the incomes of the most vulnerable households, thereby building their adaptive capacity.
● Disburses territorial development and forest landscape conservation incentives multi-annually under a
progress-based payment system, specifically rewarding performance of adaptation and mitigation initiatives.
The multi-annual approach and annual evaluation of progress was first piloted by CONAFOR for PES
schemes only. This will be replicated by the project across other forestry development support programs.
These three paradigms shifting levers will be actioned through the following project approach:
166. Piloting of climate-sensitive approach to financial incentives for forestry and rural development (Outcome 1)
The three proposed paradigm-shifting innovations described above will be first piloted within CONAFOR’s incentive
payments for forestry development through the prioritization criteria of the Operational Rules of the PADFSB.
Although these already include climate change criteria, these have been poorly operationalized to prioritize
interventions, beneficiaries, and sites for rural development support because of lack of capacities at the local level
and poor integration into planning instruments and coordination between the responsible institutions. The
prioritization and selection of interventions and sites using climate change adaptation criteria will inform climate-
focused support in rural areas with the highest vulnerability, while the multi-annual progress-based payments system
will allow for the renewal of support in the form of incentives in these areas, increasing the scale and sustainability
impacts of the climate-focused initiatives. In addition, specialized technical assistance will be provided by the project
to facilitate access of those most poor and vulnerable communities that traditionally have had less or difficult access
to CONAFOR’s support programs through a demand driven, participatory approach. This will be complemented by
the implementation of a pilot program to increase ease of access to PADFSB support by the most vulnerable
communities in the Balsas PA.
167. Bottom-up participatory territorial planning and allocation of incentives. To ensure that the paradigm shift is
achieved at a decentralized level and not top down, Regional Committees for the Integrated Management of the
Territory (RCIMTs) will be established at Forest Management Unit level to foster the participation of local
communities and all levels of government (municipality, state and national) to create awareness of climate change
impacts and co-develop adaptation solutions. Furthermore, the project will support existing and create new groups of
local facilitators, including women and youth, to strengthen community-level adaptation and mitigation initiatives and
monitor ecosystem health. This local-level bottom-up approach will promote more flexible procedures for the
channeling of development finance towards climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives. Agrarian
communities and producer organizations will prepare climate change project profiles which will be reviewed for
approval for financing by RCIMTs and CONAFOR. Once approved, the Project will provide technical assistance to
beneficiaries to design their own climate-responsive land use and investment plan. Promoting capacity building and
converging action among the involved institutions and programs is important for achieving the desired
transformational change.
168. Project’s community-centric bottom-up approach and value chain support (Outcomes 1 and 2) The project will
support bottom-up participatory processes and provide technical assistance at the community-level to improve
territorial governance and investment planning instruments for climate change adaptation in the Balsas PA, in
particular but not limited to facilitating the creation of the RCIMTs, implementing capacity development programs for
extension workers, and local facilitators and the creation of “climate change adaptation brigades”, with a focus on
women and youth. In addition, the project will build the capacities and make the investments needed to implement
climate-resilient production practices at the community level, including the enhancement of gender-inclusive value
chains and market access to support their livelihoods and raise their incomes.
169. Scaling up of the climate-sensitive rural development support prioritization process across the Balsas PA
(Outcomes 2 and 3). The project will promote the mainstreaming of the new climate-based approach into the main
rural development institutions (and their initiatives) operating in the Balsas PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA and
AGRICULTURAAGRICULTURA), through the multi-sectoral coordination platforms. This will ensure that rural

D
development support in the Balsas adopts a decentralized, coordinated and climate-responsive approach across all
sectors, and presents a model that can be scaled up across Mexico’s rural areas.
170. Lessons learned and innovation. The project has significant potential for generating lessons learned and
developing new models that integrate climate change mitigation, adaptation, livelihood resilience and climate risk
management into key forestry and agricultural policy instruments. This integration, however, will be done gradually. It
will be parallel to ongoing monitoring and evaluation process aimed at continuous improvement. These efforts will
lead to field-proved, replicable, and scalable models for climate smart forest conservation and forestland restoration
practices, which can be scaled up at the national level and in other countries in the Latin American Region.
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
171. The proposed project builds upon the Balsas-South Pacific Basin Regional Development and Welfare
Program included in the 2019-2024 National Development Plan (NDP). Project outcomes, outputs and activities will
contribute to reducing emissions linked to agriculture, forestry, and land use change, while simultaneously
strengthening the provision of ecosystem services and increasing the target population’s resilience to climate change.
These actions are directly linked to Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 – Climate action, which aims to "take
urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts". Project activities will contribute towards achieving a further 6
of the 17 SDGs, namely: SDG 1 – No poverty; SDG 2 – Zero hunger; SDG 3 – Good health and wellbeing; SDG 5 –
Gender equality; SDG 6 – Clean water and sanitation; and SDG 15 – Life on land. In addition, interventions under the
project will produce multiple environmental, social, and economic co-benefits, being the following most relevant:
● Maintenance of environmental services. A crucial environmental co-benefit of this project is the retention ,
regulation and release of rainwater flows within the ecosystems and the landscapes. Prolonged draught
periods on one hand, and more severe and heavy rainfalls on the other hand pose important hazards for local
communities and especially for the most vulnerable rural livelihoods that depend on forest products and
agriculture for which a regulated water flow and soil humidity is vital.
● Conservation of biodiversity. Within the Basin, 62% of biodiversity areas are vulnerable due to the impacts of
climate change on their plant communities80, while the expansion of agricultural activities into forested areas
is further negatively impacting biodiversity in the region. The promotion of climate-adapted forestry and
natural resource management under the project will promote the conservation of biodiversity and forest
resources, resulting in increased available habitat for wildlife, the preservation of culturally and aesthetically
important areas, and improved ecosystem service supplies, such as disease regulation.
D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Socioeconomic status
172. Despite being among the 15 largest economies in the world and second largest economy in Latin America
and the Caribbean, and having strong macroeconomic institutions, Mexico has underperformed in terms of growth,
inclusion and poverty reduction compared to similar countries over the last three decades. Average economic growth
per year between 1980 and 2018 was low at 2%, limiting progress relative to high income economies. After
contracting by 8.2% in 2020, Mexico’s economy grew by 4.8% in 202181.
173. In 2018, poverty and extreme poverty affected 41.9% (52.4 million) and 7.4% (9.3 million) of the population,
respectively82, according to the multidimensional poverty measure used by the Government of Mexico83. It is
estimated that the COVID-19 crisis resulted in a further 9.5 and 7.9 million people falling into poverty and extreme
poverty, respectively84. In addition to economic inequality and poverty, Mexican populations suffer from significant
food insecurity and nutrition challenges – among Mexico’s 7.8 million rural households, only 30.9% have an adequate
level of food security. Data from the pandemic period in 2020 show that the proportion of people suffering from
severe food insecurity increased from 9.2% in 2018 to 13%85. According to the National Council for the Evaluation of
Social Development Policy (CONEVAL), the states that make up the Balsas PA widely exceed the national average
for poverty and extreme poverty, in particular those prioritized by the proposed project (Oaxaca, Guerrero, Michoacan
and Puebla).
174. The population of 15.8 million people living in the Balsas PA is extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate
change because of its high poverty rates and limited social development levels relative to the rest of Mexico. Existing
inequities faced by women in the Balsas PA in accessing different services and assets are exacerbated by the effects
of climate change. For example, activities such as subsistence production (traspatio), housework and family care are
made more difficult due to the increase in temperatures, the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (such
as floods), water scarcity and the absence of public drinking water services. Economic activities undertaken by
women, such as the management and harvesting of non-timber forest products, are affected by the loss of vegetation
due to droughts, fires, hurricanes, and torrential rains. In addition, many of the women ejidatarios, not being owners

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) |  | 
Socioeconomic status
172. Despite being among the 15 largest economies in the world and second largest economy in Latin America
and the Caribbean, and having strong macroeconomic institutions, Mexico has underperformed in terms of growth,
inclusion and poverty reduction compared to similar countries over the last three decades. Average economic growth
per year between 1980 and 2018 was low at 2%, limiting progress relative to high income economies. After
contracting by 8.2% in 2020, Mexico’s economy grew by 4.8% in 202181.
173. In 2018, poverty and extreme poverty affected 41.9% (52.4 million) and 7.4% (9.3 million) of the population,
respectively82, according to the multidimensional poverty measure used by the Government of Mexico83. It is
estimated that the COVID-19 crisis resulted in a further 9.5 and 7.9 million people falling into poverty and extreme
poverty, respectively84. In addition to economic inequality and poverty, Mexican populations suffer from significant
food insecurity and nutrition challenges – among Mexico’s 7.8 million rural households, only 30.9% have an adequate
level of food security. Data from the pandemic period in 2020 show that the proportion of people suffering from
severe food insecurity increased from 9.2% in 2018 to 13%85. According to the National Council for the Evaluation of
Social Development Policy (CONEVAL), the states that make up the Balsas PA widely exceed the national average
for poverty and extreme poverty, in particular those prioritized by the proposed project (Oaxaca, Guerrero, Michoacan
and Puebla).
174. The population of 15.8 million people living in the Balsas PA is extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate
change because of its high poverty rates and limited social development levels relative to the rest of Mexico. Existing
inequities faced by women in the Balsas PA in accessing different services and assets are exacerbated by the effects
of climate change. For example, activities such as subsistence production (traspatio), housework and family care are
made more difficult due to the increase in temperatures, the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (such
as floods), water scarcity and the absence of public drinking water services. Economic activities undertaken by
women, such as the management and harvesting of non-timber forest products, are affected by the loss of vegetation
due to droughts, fires, hurricanes, and torrential rains. In addition, many of the women ejidatarios, not being owners |  | 

=== PAGE 63 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 59
D
of the land, cannot access programs for equipment, infrastructure, credits, or economic support for payments of
ecosystem services. Women are thus highly vulnerable to climate change impacts expected in this region,86
175. Young people in the Balsas PA make up 44-55% of the population, with rural youth representing more than
60% of the total population in the States Oaxaca and Guerrero. These rural young people lack economic
opportunities or the possibility of participating in community decision-making, leaving them with limited mechanisms
to increase their resilience to the impacts of climate change.87 Indigenous people historically have the highest rates of
poverty in Mexico: In 2016, 72% (8.2 million) of Mexico’s indigenous people lived in poverty. In 2020, over 9.6 million
indigenous people were living in poverty in the municipalities where data was available88. The situation is even more
adverse for indigenous women living in rural areas (8 out of 10 live in poverty and 4 out of 10 in extreme poverty)89.
Despite their great cultural wealth and their natural resources, the indigenous and traditional peoples have the lowest
monetary income and access to services, and have high rates of food and nutritional insecurity15, leaving them
extremely vulnerable to climate change impacts. Based on an evaluation of climate vulnerability and levels of
marginalization in the Balsas PA, the project will prioritize eight watersheds and two indigenous territories for a total
of 121 municipalities and respond to the needs of ACs, small producers, women, youth, people with disabilities and
indigenous peoples, helping them to better confront the harm, risks and effects they are exposed to because of
extreme weather events and climate change.
Absence of alternative sources of finance
176. As already described in Section B.5., while the Government of Mexico (CONAFOR in particular) continue to
invest in the country’s forestry sector there are several obstacles preventing the flow of tailored funding for climate
change adaptation and mitigation in the Balsas PA. These include: i) current financial support is not being specifically
focused on the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and is not informed by climate scenarios to support
climate adaptive sustainable forest ecosystem management; ii) limited access to credit for promoting the inclusion of
small producers (especially women and youth who are usually not the owners of land in agrarian communities) in
value chains related to timber, non-timber and agroforestry products; iii) lack of inclusion of women and youth in
ecosystem management and value chain activities, which negatively affects the sustainability of medium- to long-term
adaptation needs; and iv) despite the current PES support provided by CONAFOR and CONAGUA, the end users of
ecosystem services do not sufficiently contribute to the maintenance of the relevant ecosystems. External investment
is therefore required to provide the concessionally needed and to finance the existing funding gap for adaptation and
mitigation activities in the Balsas PA.
Institutional capacity needs
177. There is currently a weak level of existing climate governance in the Balsas PA. This is partly because efforts
to integrate a climate appro ach into public policies have only recently begun. Furthermore, institutional coordination
among the involved actors and specialized technical assistance on climate change have been limited. As a result,
there have been no clearly focused incentives encouraging concerted action for climate change mitigation and
adaptation. The main institutions working in the Balsas PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and
AGRICULTURAAGRICULTURA) have been developing advanced information systems related to weather forecast,
forest fires, medium/long term evolution of climate patterns and climate risk prevention at central level. However,
capacity building at regional and local level is needed to improve climate information sharing and uptake by
communities, municipalities, and extension services.

D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
178. This project has been developed in collaboration with multiple institutions and stakeholders under the lead of
the CONAFOR and the guidance of the NDA, the Mexican Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (Secretaría de
Hacienda y Crédito Público SCHP). The Office for the Coordination of Advisers to the Presidency of the Republic
(CAP) has facilitated smooth inter-institutional collaboration with CONAGUA and AGRICULTURAAGRICULTURA,
while IFAD and the FAO Investment Centre have supported the drafting of this project that has involved a
participatory, consultative, and multi-sectoral planning process. Through this process, a paradigm shift to current
institutional modalities and development models have been identified that foster low emissions and increase
adaptation capacity and resilience of the most vulnerable rural livelihoods. The Project is completely aligned with and
has been designed to implement Mexico´s climate change, environmental and social policies, and priorities,
specifically:
● Mexico's Nationally Determined Contributions: 2020 update90 (NDCs) respond to both mitigation and
adaptation problems experienced in the country across multiple sectors. Using a cross-cutting approach, this
project has the potential to contribute concurrently to both climate change mitigation and adaptation targets in
Mexico’s NDCs. According to the NDCs, mitigation activity in the land use, land use change and forestry
sector (uso del suelo, cambio de uso del suelo y silvicultura USCUSS) directly aligns with the overarching
aim of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA). The NDCs recognize the need to incorporate climate criteria into
