=== PAGE 64 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 60
D
rural development programs, as well as to implement environmental protection and land use standards. The
project framework aims to contribute to the NDCs’ goals firstly in terms of Climate Change Adaptation,
specifically in axes A, B, C and D . This will involve activities focusing on: i) social sector adaptation to
climate change — all project outp9u1ts; ii) EbA — Output 2.1 (climate-sensitive management of ecosystems);
and iii) water resource and supply management (Output 2.2). Contributions will occur secondly in terms of
Climate Change Mitigation. This will involve reducing emissions through activities focusing on the: (a) land
use sector via land use change and forestry activities (e.g., afforestation, reforestation, forest management,
farmland management and pasture management) — Outputs 2.1 and 2.2 (increased commercialization and
added value of climate-resilient forestry, agroforestry, silvo-pastoral and backyard production systems and
their products); and (b) agricultural sector via soil management, scheduled soil burns and manure
management — Output 2.2. A detailed matrix that shows how project activities are aligned within the NDCs is
presented in Annex 2.
● Mexico’s National Climate Change Strategy (2013)92 (Estrategia Nacional de Cambio Climático / ENACC)
sets the long-term planning for climate change in the country. The proposed project will contribute to the
following strategic adaptation (A) and mitigation (M) axes in the ENACC: i) reducing vulnerability and
increasing social sector resilience to climate change (lines of action A1.7 and A1.11) — all project outputs; ii)
reducing vulnerability and increasing strategic infrastructure and production-system resilience to climate
change (line of action A2.6) — Outputs 2.1 and 2.2; iii) sustainably using and protecting ecosystems while
preserving the environmental services they provide (lines of action A3.1, A3.8 and A3.9) — Outputs 2.1 and
2.2; and iv) promoting agricultural and forestry best practices in order to increase and sustain natural carbon
sinks (lines of action M4.1, M4.2, M4.7, M4.8, M4.10, M4.11, M4.12 and M4.13) — Outputs 2.1 and 2.2.
● The Special Program on Climate Change 2021–202493 (Programa Especial de Cambio Climático/PECC)
relates to short-term planning for climate change in the country. This document includes targets for 2030 to
be achieved by the development and implementation of 169 specific actions under four priority objectives:
adaptation; mitigation; co-benefits derived from synergies between adaptation and mitigation; and climate
governance. The proposed project aligns with the PECC through the following lines of action: 1.1.9, 1.3.6,
1.3.7, 2.7.1, 2.7.2, 2.7.3, 2.7.6, 3.1.8, 4.2., 4.3.8 and 4.6.8.
● Mexico’s National REDD+ Strategy for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation
2017–203094 (Estrategia Nacional de Reducción de Emisiones por Deforestación y Degradación
Forestal/ENAREDD+) has the following objectives: (i) to increase forest area via sustainable management,
natural and induced forest regeneration and forest conservation; (ii) to continue developing the social and
economic capital of rural communities; and (iii) to increase sustainable best-practices in production at the
rural landscape level. The proposed project will contribute to these objectives through improved climate-
resilient and low-emission management of ecosystems to enhance service supplies, as well as through
climate-sensitive production systems and value chains to diversify. This will include improved protection of
forests and prioritization of rural agrarian communities. The Project will support the implementation of all
seven components of this Strategy, as shown in the matrix included in Annex 2a..
● Project activities will directly support the National Forestry Program 2020–202495 (Programa Nacional
Forestal 2020–2024), which has the main objective to promote community forest management for sustainable
and diversified use of forest resources, as well as the integration and development of local networks and
competitive values that trigger local economies to improve the quality of life of the population living in forest
areas. Activities under Outputs 2.1 and 2.2 will contribute directly to the program’s objectives.
● The General Law on Climate Change (2012)96 (Ley General de Cambio Climático / LGCC) aims to align
activities on mitigation and adaptation between different government entities. The law established the
following institutions: the National Institute of Ecology and Climate Change; the Climate Change Council; and
the Inter-Secretariat Commission for Climate Change. It also created a fund to channel financial resources
(public, private, national, and international) to support actions to combat climate change. The law was
amended in November 2020 to comply with the Paris Agreement and the NDCs. The proposed project will
comply with all directives included in the LGCC, to ensure that adaptation and mitigation activities
implemented in the Balsas PA are effectively coordinated between government actors at all levels. This will
include the strengthening of institutional coordination mechanisms for climate-driven rural development in the
Balsas PA (Output 1.2).
● This cross-cutting project aligns with the goals and commitments in Mexico’s most recent National
Development Plan 2019-202497 (NDP) (Plan Nacional de Desarrollo 2019–2024), both in terms of mitigation
and adaptation98. One of the main principles of this plan is the promotion of the efficient and responsible use
of resources, which activities under Outputs 1.2 and 2.1 will contribute to. In addition, activities under Output
2.2 will directly contribute to specific objectives in the NDP, including: i) the stimulation of innovation,
competition and integration of all value chains and the generation of higher value-added activities in all
productive sectors of the economy in an environmentally conscious and sustainable manner; and ii) the

Institutions involved and stakeholder engagement
179. The capacity, experience, and the roles of IFAD as the GCF Accredited Entity (AE), CONAFOR as the
Project Executing Entity undertaking all operational responsibility for the implementation of the Project (EE), and
NAFIN and HACIENDA as Executing Entities channeling the funds to CONAFOR as well as the other project partners
have been extensively described in section B.4 Implementation Arrangements above and will therefore not be
repeated in this section.
180. Under the guidance of CONAFOR and the CAP a mission to México City, Guadalajara and the Balsas PA
was undertaken by an IF AD/FAO project appraisal and preparation team in June 2022. The objective of the mission
was togather relevant information required for the preparation of the Funding Proposal with contributions from
CONAFOR, CAP, HACIENDA, CONAGUA, and other government and civil society institutions operating in the
project area. In addition, the mission provided the opportunity to identify and visit a diversity of actors, communities
and experiences in the Balsas PA that could inform best practices for stakeholder engagement and participation in
the project. Mission participants visited several ejidos and indigenous communities who have already received
support from CONAFOR’s PADFSB. These visits provided a better understanding of how the PADFSB operates, as
well as an opportunity to gather diverse views from different rural development actors about community enterprises,
livelihoods, development possibilities and the effects of climate change at local level. Specific focus was given to
initiatives prioritizing women and youth, asthe active and visible participation of these groups is recognized as an
ongoing challenge. Through meetings held at CONAFOR headquarters in Guadalajara,the organizational structure,
strategic priorities, and implementation arra ngements for the project were discussed and agreed upon and a
Stakeholder Participation Plan designed (see Annex 7.b).
181. During the months of September and October 2022 several meaningful consultation workshops were
organized by CONAFOR with indigenous communities an d local actors in the project region. These consultation
meetings have been documented as part of in the Indigenous Peoples Consultation and Participation Plan included in
Annex 6 and are summarized in the following table.

D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
182. The Economic and Financial Analysis (please see also Annex 3) shows that this project is economically
viable as a whole. Main sources of quantifiable benefits result from the investments in Outcome 2 and include the
increase in productivity, resilience of the productive systems to climate change, and mitigation benefits from GHG
emission reductions. Main sources of benefits in Outcomes 1 and 3 include enabling and supporting activities for the
correct implementation and monitoring of the project investments, which also increase the adaptation benefits of the
project, at a wider scale, namely the watershed scale, and are not directly quantified in this analysis, rendering its
results rather conservative.
183. The benefit analysis focuses on the investments, where forest landscapes are sustainably managed and
better adapted to climate change for the provision of environmental services and increased livelihood resilience and
its activities include: 2.1.1 Payment of Environmental Services (PES), 2.2.1 Climate Smart Forest Landscape
Restoration of Micro-watersheds and Strategic Areas (RFM), 2.2.2 Commercial Forest and Agroforestry Plantations
Adapted to Climate Change (PFCA), 2.3.1 Sustainable Forest Management adapted to climate change, inclusion,
and access to markets (MFCVV), and 2.3.2 Support households to improve food security and nutrition. Models for

The economic results at the aggregate level of the project are estimated based on the area (in hectares) or projects
estimated to be completed by the project. Importantly, this step also includes the benefits related to climate change
mitigation through the impact on GHG emissions reduction estimated with the Ex-Act tool for the different models
(see Annex 22). Three price scenarios were used to include the mitigation benefits from the World Bank guide for the
social valuation of carbon, starting at approximately USD 42/tCO2eq for the low-price scenario up to USD 84/tCO2eq for
the high scenario. The discount rates used to estimate the economic net present value (NPVe) were 6% and 10%.
Table 11 summarizes the results for the carbon price scenarios, as well as for the 2 discount rates. Although the
important adaptation benefits are not included, the economic rate of return (IRRe) as well as the net present value for
the discount rates used are positive.

 | the high scenario. The discount rates used to estimate the economic net present value (NPVe) were 6% and 10%. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

184. Sensitivity analysis. A sensitivity analysis was carried out for following different scenarios: (i) a reduction in
net benefits due to the combination of risks in sale prices; (ii) productive yields; (iii) levels of adoption; (iv) survival of
the ventures; (v) an increase in costs due, for example, to the increase in prices of inputs and increases in expenses;
(vi) delays in starting the project or in incorporating beneficiaries; and (vii) mixed scenarios of these phenomena. The
results of the sensitivity analysis indicate that the economic results of the project remain attractive even under the
simulated unfavorable conditions, showing a positive NPV in all the scenarios considered. Detailed assumptions and
calculations are attached in Annex 3. Table 12 below presents the main results of the sensitivity test.

185. Regarding other efficiency and effectiveness indicators, the co-financing ratio of the of the GCF funds versus
the government and IFAD funds in the project is 1:1.3, which is pertinent for the country and is evidence that the GCF
is not the major source of funding for the project. The cost for the project per direct beneficiary amounts to 364 USD,
while the costs per indirect beneficiary is about 24 USD. Although the project is not directly focused on mitigation it
still achieves a cost of 18.7 USD/ton CO2eq a value that is almost half of the lowest social price of carbon.

contributes as a whole, including in respect of any co-financing.

E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1-2 pages)
This section of the logical framework is meant to help a project/programme monitor and assess how it contributes to the paradigm shift described in section D.2 above by applying
three assessment dimensions - scale, replicability, and sustainability.
Accordingly, for each assessment dimension (see the definition per assessment in the accompanying guidance note), describe the current state (baseline) and the potential scenario
(target) and rate the current state (baseline) by using the three-point-scale rating (low, medium, and high) provided in the guidance note. Also describe how the project/programme
will contribute to that shift/ transformation under respective assessment dimensions (scale, replicability and sustainability). In doing so, please refer to section B.2(a) (theory of
change).

benefits. As a result, integral territorial
development support in the PA will adopt
a decentralized, coordinated and climate-
responsive approach across all sectors,
and presents a model that can be scaled
up across Mexico’s rural areas.
Replicability | An explicit model for
climate-resilient and
low-emission
development for
production systems in
the forestry and
agriculture sectors in
Mexico’s has not yet
been proven. As such,
there is no proof of
concept for replication
by multisectoral
coordination structures.
W | Low | The project’s paradigm shift has the
potential to be replicated across Mexico:
i.) Through the operationalization of
climate change criteria in the prioritization
of rural development incentives and; ii.)
through the strengthening of multisectoral
coordination structures to re-align other
sources of national development finance
towards climate finance, such as those of
CONAGUA. These approaches are the
basis of the project’s financial exit
strategy and its potential for replicability
in Mexico. | Piloting of an incentivized climate-sensitive
approach to integral territorial development
will first take place within CONAFOR’s
Reglas de Operación. In addition,
specialized technical assistance will be
provided by the project to facilitate
access of those most poor and vulnerable
communities that traditionally have had
less or difficult access to CONAFOR’s
support programs through a demand
driven, participatory approach. This will
be complemented by the implementation
of a pilot program to increase ease of
access to PADFSB support by the most
vulnerable communities in the Basin. To
ensure that the paradigm shift is achieved
at a decentralized level and not top down,
multisectoral platforms will be established
at the sub-basin and ethnic territory level
to foster the participation of local
communities and all levels of
government. Furthermore, the project will
support existing and create new local
facilitators. This local-level bottom-up
approach will promote more flexible
procedures for the channeling of
development finance to climate change
initiatives via Basin and Sub-Basin

18 6. During project implementation, CONAFOR will be responsible for overall Pr oject monitoring. The project will recruit a full time Monitoring and Evaluati on (M&E)
expert who will collaborate with the UCP and ETP of CONAFOR. The Project’s progress will be measured against the indicators included in the Project Logical
Framework above. A project-specific Monitoring and Evaluation plan will put together according to the approved Logical Framework. The plan includes a household
baseline and at least two external evaluations during Project implementation (at midterm and final review), a permanent strategy to collect and analyze information
related to the implementation of the Project. The final review will assess the achievement of indicators, sustainability of results, and lessons learned. The parameters
of such evaluations will be outlined in the Operation Manual.
187. Although impact indicators are essentially related to the improvement in the capacity in adopting new climate-resilient forest landscape management and
livelihood strategies, the Logical Framework also includes substantial GHG emission reduction benefits. At the impact, outcome, and output levels, the indicators
disaggregate targets by gender, age, and ethnic composition of the beneficiaries. The M&E system will also measure improvement in terms of the development of new
institutional instruments and mechanisms oriented to mainstream climate change in policies, operational rules of national programs, and coordination efforts among
the different levels of the Government. The Logical Framework also includes indicators that reflect the progress of the Project in terms of the forest landscape areas
that are managed according to new climate change criteria and nature-based solutions. In addition, a set of deliverables for each activity is identified to enable the
project team to follow up on the progress of the different outcomes of the Project. Each Multiannual Invest Plan supported in the prioritized areas will also include basic
indicators, whose progress will trigger the corresponding annual financial and technical supports.
188. The collection of M&E data in the field and at the CONAFOR level will combine different systems and activities:
The Integrated Information System of Supports of CONAFOR (“Sistema Integral de Información de Apoyos” SIIAC for the Spanish acronym) collects
o
and records information on the proposals formulated by potential beneficiaries, the evaluation of these proposals and the supported actions by the
PADFSB in the rural communities, including the Multi- Annual Investment Plans. SIACC comprises a geospatial application that allows for the
localization of the beneficiaries, the intervened polygons, and the delimitation of the rural communities. SIIAC will be a major information system to
collect, analyse, and report information related to the beneficiaries and actions supported by the project.
Regarding overlapping / double counting of beneficiaries: To grant and incentive payment within the PADFSB, CONAFOR signs a legal agreement
o
with the benefitting Agrarian Community or group. This legal agreement has to include a list of the heads of the household that are going to benefit
from the supported activities, including their names and their personal identification number “Clave Única del Registro de Población” (CURP). This
information is registered within CONAFORs´ information system for PADFSB forest incentives, the SIICA, which allows CONAFOR for full traceability
of the granted incentives. There are cases in which a household benefits from more than one activity, as some are synergistic and/or complementary.
In those cases, CONAFOR eliminates any possible double counting or duplication by using the unique personal identification number CURP, as its
holder is counted as only one beneficiary for the CONAFOR.
Household survey. At the beginning and the final of the project, a household survey will be carried out to collect social, cultural, and economic
o
information on a probabilistic sample of potential project beneficiaries, who will be later divided between control and treatment groups. This survey
aims to obtain quantitative and qualitative information to measure improvement in climate resilience, livelihood, and adoption of new practices, as well
as gender and ethnic disaggregated information.
The project will follow the IFAD’s Guidelines to measure several corporative Core Indicators (CI) that were incorporated in the Balsas logical framework.
o
The CIs consist of 1 outreach, 20 output and 22 outcome indicators thatare mapped to the strategic objectives (SOs) and areas of thematic focus of
IFAD Strategic Framework 2016-2025; they are also aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) defined in the 2030 Agenda.
The Local Brigades for Measuring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) is a new CONAFOR initiative that will be piloted through the Balsas project and
o
then scaled up at the national level. Within the project area, 18 brigades formed by one local expert and two rural youth (men and women) will be
recruited by CONAFOR to participate in the data collection for the National Forest Inventory, the estimation of the deforestation rate, as well as the

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)
Selected Risk Factor 1
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Low Medium
Description
Low annual project budget allocations and/or the failure to allocate resources in accordance with the project timeline
can seriously disrupt disbursements and operations.
Mitigation Measure(s)
IFAD’s experience in Mexico has shown national systems to be advanced and reliable, which will ensure that the
probability of this risk occurring remains low. To further mitigate this risk and ensure that project implementation is not
adversely affected, IFAD as AE will work closely with CONAFOR (EE) to promptly allocate sufficient budgetary
resources over the project life cycle.
Selected Risk Factor 2
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Medium
Description
Lack of coordination between the various state agencies involved in project coordination (including CONAFOR,
AGRICULTURA, SEMARNAT, BIENESTAR, FIRA and INPI amongst others). This is linked to a lack of platforms to
foster dialogue.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will contribute to building the institutional capacities of these stakeholders by strengthening inter-
institutional coordination at the federal, state and municipal level. In addition, the project will ensure regular
engagements take place with the institutional stakeholders and ensure that platforms are in place to bring them
together for dialogue and planning related to project implementation. The risk will also be mitigated through close
coordination with and support from the Office for the Coordination of Advisers to the Presidency of the Republic,
HACIENDA and the implementing partners.
Selected Risk Factor 3
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Medium
Description
Economic crises may arise as a result of the disasters such as the COVID-19 pandemic. This, in turn, could lead to
reduced government revenue and thus, limited public investment in initiatives and incentives for rural production or
forest landscape conservation activities, including PES.
Mitigation Measure(s)
In the case of unavoidable disasters, such as the COVID-19 pandemic, which may result in economic crises,
investment in rural production and forest landscape conservation activities will be safeguarded through
predetermined integral territorial budgets allocated in advance by CONAFOR. CONAFOR’s integral territorial budgets
are purposed to support and protect economic development in rural areas, including during economic crises (such as
that caused by the COVID-19 pandemic), which will contribute to mitigating this risk.
Selected Risk Factor 4
Category Probability Impact

=== PAGE 90 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 86
E
Forex Medium High
Description
Fluctuations in the exchange range, particularly devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the US dollar, negatively
impact project budget planning, disbursement and spending, which may disrupt the implementation of the project.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The GCF funding will be disbursed quarterly throughout the project’s term, in the historically and presumptively more
stable currency of the US Dollar, which will mitigate the impact of any devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the
dollar. In its Co-financing agreements, IFAD expects to require CONAFOR to hold this Co-financing in Mexican peso
denominated accounts to allow for an onshore account. Although the project’s financial model is articulated in US
Dollars, most costs will eventually be incurred in Mexican pesos, with currency conversion to take place upon receipt
by CONAFOR for project costs. The Operating Manual governing CONAFOR’s management of these funds
incorporates a strategy to mitigate exchange rate risk. The project financial model also includes a contingency fund
that may be available to mitigate the impact of currency fluctuation.
Selected Risk Factor 5
Category Probability Impact
Other Low Medium
Description
The project may face a change in the Federal Government, shifting priorities in the target region and resulting in
varied institutional support during the implementation period.
Mitigation Measure(s)
This risk will be mitigated by aligning the project with state programmes aimed at climate change mitigation and
poverty reduction — both during project development and implementation. This will ensure that the project is tightly
aligned with government priorities at all times during the implementation period. Similarly, the project will align with
other platforms and public contexts which may foster opportunities for relevant dialogue with stakeholder, to ensure
continued support.
Selected Risk Factor 6
Category Probability Impact
Other Medium High
Description
Forest fires (caused by humans) and extreme weather events (such as floods) may result in damage to or loss of
forest cover, negatively affecting the progress and success of reforestation activities.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Selected Risk Factor 7
Category Probability Impact
Other Medium Medium
Description
Organized crime in the Project Region may hinder or delay project implementation and/or put project personnel at
risk.
Mitigation Measure(s)
.
Military intelligence of the Government of Mexico informs the CONAFOR, as well as all other government institutions,
about the presence and the situation relative of organized crime in their respective region. CONAFOR´s local field
offices (“promotorías) obtain direct information about the security situation so that dangerous situations can be
avoided and the security of CONAFOR´s technical staff is granted. The project will reinforce the communication

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
189. To identify environmental and social risks and propose appropriate mitigation measures, an Environmental
and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared for the project (Annex 6). Based on the assessment
conducted, the project is categorized as a Category B or ‘moderate risk’ according to the GCF project classification.
This categorization was selected because any potential environmental and social impacts related to activities
(especially under Outcome 2) will be localized and easily reversible through already known and commonly used
mitigation measures. All sub-projects under Outcome 2 will be screened to ensure a Category B risk level or lower
and abbreviated Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs) to screen and monitor eventual impacts will
be prepared.
190. CONAFOR has an adequate institutional framework to assess and manage potential social and
environmental impacts. CONAFOR´s operational rules (Reglas de Operación - ROs) and manuals (guías) are used to
screen and monitor community project proposals and prevent the funding of any socially or environmentally harmful
activities as per Mexican Law and safeguard requirements. The project will strengthen these ROs and CONAFOR´s
capacities. CONAFOR has an extensive experience in safeguards gained by working on the design and
implementation of diverse programs and activities with IFAD, the World Bank, the Forest Investment Program, the Bio
Carbon Fund, and bilateral cooperation with Norway and others. Its safeguard institutional framework is also aligned
with the GCF´s safeguards requirements. CONAFOR has successfully managed loans of over USD 500 million and
grants of approximately USD 25 million without any significant safeguard issues.
191. The project seeks to increase the resilience of vulnerable forest-dependent communities and the ecosystems
they depend on. In terms of Indigenous Peoples of the Tlapaneco, Zapoteco, Amuzgo, Mixteco, Chatino, Tacuate,
Triqui, Nahua and Purépecha ethnic groups and other vulnerable groups, the greatest risk identified is the possibility
that their positive and active role in climate action is not adequately recognized by focusing narrowly on them as mere
beneficiaries rather than on their potential leadership. In this sense, the most significant risks revolve around:
● Inadequate indigenous and vulnerable groups’ participation.
● Marginalization and erosion of Indigenous and vulnerable groups’ aspirations, capacities, knowledge,
practices, and perspectives; specifically:
Indigenous planning practices
o
Indigenous knowledge around land stewardship
o
Indigenous governance practices
o
Indigenous economies of reciprocity
o
192. The project´s environmental risks and impacts on biodiversity and natural habitats are very small, introduction
of invasive species, use of forbidden pesticides, undue use of natural resources are prevented at the inception phase
of sub-project through the use of CONAFOR´s screening tools and exclusion list. To ensure proper implementation of
these restrictions project extension agents will receive additional capacity building for safeguards implementation and
monitoring.
193. A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) to handle eventual questions, complaints, suggestions, access to
information requests, comments and grievances is already in place at CONAFOR (called the Mecanismo de Acción
Ciudadana, MAC). The operation of the MAC is carried out in three different areas that have their own channels,
regulations, and attention protocols, depending on the nature of the matters to resolve and/or attend, namely: (i)
Internal Control Body (includes the Social Comptrollers); (ii) Liaison Unit - INAI (National Institute for Access to
Information); and (iii) Citizen Information and Attention Service (SIAC). If any project-related grievances cannot be
resolved by CONAFOR’s MAC, they will be elevated to the level of IFAD’s GRM/Complaints procedure. IFAD
Complaints Procedure exists to ensure that appropriate mechanisms are in place to allow individuals and
communities to contact IFAD directly and file a complaint if they believe they are or may be adversely affected by the
project not complying with IFAD’s social and environmental policies. Complainants are thus encouraged to first bring
the matter to the attention of CONAFOR as the EE but may bring the issue directly to IFAD if they feel the EE has not
adequately responded or if they feel they might be subject to retaliation if they approach the EE directly.
194. Coordination of safeguards monitoring will be the responsibility of the Gerencia de Biodiversidad y Cambio
Climático (GBCC). In order to complement GCBB supervision capacity at the field level, safeguards monitoring will
also be undertaken by the Promotorías Forestales (PF) and its staff, who will receive capacity building for this
purpose. The GBCC will be in charge of overseeing training and capacity building at the PF to ensure proper
safeguards implementation and monitoring. The PF will monitor safeguards implementation at the field level ensuring
compliance with safeguard requirements.
195. Relevant documentation related to safeguards will be disclosed in a timely manner. For Category B projects,
disclosure shall be at least 30 days before the expected date of approval of the GCF in accordance with the
requirements of the GCF Disclosure Policy. Sub-projects at the state level will disclose all relevant documentation at
least 30 days before the expected date of approval by CONAFOR. Documents disclosed will be presented in an

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
189. To identify environmental and social risks and propose appropriate mitigation measures, an Environmental
and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared for the project (Annex 6). Based on the assessment
conducted, the project is categorized as a Category B or ‘moderate risk’ according to the GCF project classification.
This categorization was selected because any potential environmental and social impacts related to activities
(especially under Outcome 2) will be localized and easily reversible through already known and commonly used
mitigation measures. All sub-projects under Outcome 2 will be screened to ensure a Category B risk level or lower
and abbreviated Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs) to screen and monitor eventual impacts will
be prepared.
190. CONAFOR has an adequate institutional framework to assess and manage potential social and
environmental impacts. CONAFOR´s operational rules (Reglas de Operación - ROs) and manuals (guías) are used to
screen and monitor community project proposals and prevent the funding of any socially or environmentally harmful
activities as per Mexican Law and safeguard requirements. The project will strengthen these ROs and CONAFOR´s
capacities. CONAFOR has an extensive experience in safeguards gained by working on the design and
implementation of diverse programs and activities with IFAD, the World Bank, the Forest Investment Program, the Bio
Carbon Fund, and bilateral cooperation with Norway and others. Its safeguard institutional framework is also aligned
with the GCF´s safeguards requirements. CONAFOR has successfully managed loans of over USD 500 million and
grants of approximately USD 25 million without any significant safeguard issues.
191. The project seeks to increase the resilience of vulnerable forest-dependent communities and the ecosystems
they depend on. In terms of Indigenous Peoples of the Tlapaneco, Zapoteco, Amuzgo, Mixteco, Chatino, Tacuate,
Triqui, Nahua and Purépecha ethnic groups and other vulnerable groups, the greatest risk identified is the possibility
that their positive and active role in climate action is not adequately recognized by focusing narrowly on them as mere
beneficiaries rather than on their potential leadership. In this sense, the most significant risks revolve around:
● Inadequate indigenous and vulnerable groups’ participation.
● Marginalization and erosion of Indigenous and vulnerable groups’ aspirations, capacities, knowledge,
practices, and perspectives; specifically:
Indigenous planning practices
o
Indigenous knowledge around land stewardship
o
Indigenous governance practices
o
Indigenous economies of reciprocity
o

accessible and culturally appropriate manner, accounting for the specific needs of Indigenous and community gro ups
that may be affected by the implementation of the project (such as literacy, gender, language differences,

196. During the consultation process with the representatives of the Agrarian Communities (ACs) of the prioritized
indigenous municipalities of the Purépecha, Amuzgo, Tlapaneco, Mixtec and Nahua peoples, emphasis was placed
on mechanisms and forms of resolution of possible conflicts generated from the implementation of the project to
safeguard the communities. The participants highlighted the importance of the community assembly as the highest
institution for resolving internal conflicts and decision-making, as well as the importance of ensuring the participation
of institutions such as CONAFOR in the community assemblies to clearly inform communities about the objectives
and activities of the project. To do so, a complete set of activities, including a free and prior informed consultation
(FPIC) plan, has been developed (see Annex 7a) to ensure Indigenous communities’ participation in project
development and to prevent any potential conflict during implementation.
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
197. In line with GCF's gender policies, the project stimulates climate investments that contribute to reducing
gender gaps and promotes equality through climate change mitigation and adaptation measures. To this end, a
Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAP) (please see also Annex 8) was carried out that shows, that in the
Mexican countryside and specifically in the project area women are more affected by poverty than men, as 56% of
women are poor99. Women represent only 26% of landowners and gender stereotypes and machismo are
determining factors in their living conditions and their possibilities for economic and social empowerment. Among the
most relevant gender gaps are the long working hours, the invisibility of their contributions to production, conservation
of the environment, nutrition, and the family economy. Women have higher levels of illiteracy100, are less connected
than men to the mass media101 and hold much fewer102 decision-making positions in ejidos and communities. Only
one third of women in agriculture are paid for their work and have less access to credit and technical assistance.
198. Women are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change. Household activities become more burdensome
due to rising temperatures and scarcity (or excess) of water, increasing the difficulties of washing, cleaning, and
cooking. The occurrence of pathogenic diseases and the limited availability of food for the family also add to women's
psychological and work burdens. Traspatio subsistence production and economic activities such as harvesting of
non-forest timber products, which are also often carried out by women, are affected by the loss of vegetation. More
women are the heads of households due to the labour migration of men and are in charge of agricultural production,
but they lack recognition of their labour and land ownership. Inequity in access to services and goods for production
and resilience to climate change, affect women's performance and increase their vulnerability.
199. Based on these findings, recommendations were formulated in the GAP to ensure women's participation in
project activities and benefits by defining quotas or participation targets, capacity building for gender equality for the
different groups of project stakeholders. The GAP describes existing national laws and policies, strategies and plans
of the institutions involved in the project. The GAP is elaborated based on the project's expected results and outputs
framework and defines activities, indicators, and targets for women's participation. Through its implementation,
women are expected to be active participants and beneficiaries of increased social and climate resilience, improved
climate adaptation of productive ecosystems and rural community development. Furthermore the GAP:
● convenes and strengthens women's participation in decision-making in RCIMTs for the prioritization of
intervention areas as well as in the elaboration of participatory multi-annual investment plans
● promotes greater gender equity in institutional work by adjusting sectoral and/or institutional policies,
strategies, and regulations
● includes women as beneficiaries of investment plans in basins and micro-basins and for this purpose
develops communication and awareness campaigns to disseminate calls for projects with inclusive language
and based on profiles of the target population according to gender
● prepares simplified formats for the submission of project proposals
● proposes training in gender and inclusion for extensionists, local facilitators, brigadistas and promotores
forestales, and it´s up-take into CONAFOR's capacity building programme
● proposes to prioritize the participation of women in ejido and communal authorities and to use selection
criteria for Payment of Environmental Services (PES) projects that favours women's participation
● promotes that women participate in projects to improve the productive systems of the farming communities,
be trained in production practices and technologies adapted to climate change and variability, and benefit
from the financial transfers made by the project to AC´s for the hiring of technical assistance, provision of
inputs and compensation of labour costs
● proposes that climate-resilient sustainable forest management activities open-up opportunities for women's
participation in value chains, especially in economic activities linked to sustainable use and value addition to
timber and non-timber forest products, strengthening their capacities on gender, sustainable and climate-
resilient production practices, organizational, managerial, and marketing strengthening

200. The GAP includes indicators and targets for monitoring and evaluation. It also provides for the availability of

G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
201. The project's financial management (FM) will be carried out in accordance with the legal framework
governing public administration in Mexico. FM will be the responsibility of the International Affairs and Financial
Promotion Unit (UAIFF) of CONAFOR; the UCP will contribute to FM and will have a Finance specialist who will work
closely with the UAIFF.
202. NAFIN, as the Financial Agent designated by the Ministry of Finance, will be responsible for the
administration of the financing. It will follow-up on audits, managing disbursements, and will be in charge of loan
disbursement processes that the GCF will channel through IFAD via NAFIN and TESOFE to CONAFOR.
203. CONAFOR with its Fondo Forestal Mexicano will follow-up on audits, managing disbursements, and will be in
charge of grant disbursement processes that the GCF will channel through IFAD to CONAFOR.
204. For disbursements of GCF Loan A and GCF Loan B, the reimbursement method will be used given that
project expenses are pre-financed by the Government. For grants, a replenishment mechanism could be used based
on an advance payment. The bank accounts will be those defined by NAFIN, from which funds are transferred to the
Treasury of the Federation (TESOFE) in the case of the loans, and to CONAFOR in the case of the grant. The flow of
funds to beneficiaries is controlled by CONAFOR Integrated Support System (SIIAC) and by Integral administration
System (SAI) for other expenses. IFAD's Client Portal will be the online platform used by NAFIN and CONAFOR to
submit disbursement requests of the GCF Loan A, GCF Loan B and GCF grant proceeds, respectively, to IFAD.
205. The internal audit will be carried out by the Internal Control Organ (OIC) of CONAFOR, and the external audit
will be carried out by private firms to be appointed by the SFP applying the terms of reference agreed with the WB
and IDB, accepted by IFAD. IFAD's responsibility will consist of on-site supervision missions to assess FM
arrangements, identify areas requiring improvement, fiduciary risks, and corresponding mitigation measures.
206. Procurement activities will be carried out in accordance with Mexico's national procurement system. The
procurement methods will be those established in those regulations and the maximum thresholds per method will be
those established for each fiscal year in the Federal Expenditure Budget (PEF). IFAD will maintain the review prior to
the processes based on the thresholds resulting from the application of the Procurement Risk Matrix (PRM). A risk
assessment was undertaken in August 2021 during the design and it concluded that Mexico’s procurement rules are
aligned with international standards. This was confirmed in the last supervision mission for the IFAD loan to Mexico in
2023.
207. Procurement planning will be done through IFAD's current model procurement plan (PP), which will indicate,
among others, the selection methods, and the type of review by IFAD (prior or post-review) to which the processes
will be subject. The first PP will be for the first year of operation of the project and subsequent PPs will be annual.
The PIM includes the need of providing an 18-months PP in the first year of the project.
208. Requests for non-objection, both to the PP and its modifications, as well as to the procurement processes
established in the PP as subject to prior review, will be processed through the No Objection Tracking Utility System
(NOTUS). Contracts arising from procurements raised in the PP will be registered in IFAD´s Client Portal (ICP)
Contract Management Tool (CMT) platform. Procurement management will be reviewed and evaluated by IFAD
during supervision missions which will be carried out periodically, but at least twice a year.
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
● full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and

An ejido is a form of land tenure that combines communal ownership with individual use. The ejido consists of cultivated land, pastureland, other uncultivated
lands, and the town. (Being part of an ejido no longer necessarily in“vnoúlvcleeso cso amgrmaruinoas l( oNwA)n”ership. After the reform of article 27 of the Constitution and the PROCEDE
program many ejidos have privatized some or all of their land, but, unless they are formally dissolved as such, they continue to exist legally as ejidos.)

to families at different times during its development; in this system, crop rotation and their association maintain soil fertility and reduce erosion. The main axis of the
polyculture is the maize native races, accompanied by various plants, some planted and others induced or tolerated.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP236
Proposal name: Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation
Strategies (BALSAS)
Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)
Country: Mexico
Project/programme size: Medium
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1.
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following
Strengths Points of caution
remarks:
This project introduces a new integrated The project has the potential to unlock up to
territorial development approach by using USD 1.5 billion in domestic public finance by
climate change related criteria for selecting aligning federal rural development subsidies
and prioritizing the most vulnerable with climate priorities. However, achieving
beneficiaries, interventions and sites. the USD 1.5 billion figure depends on the
federal government scaling up the new
approach to allocation of rural development
funds, which lies outside the scope of the
project.
The BALSAS project’s sustainability will be
increased by using and strengthening
existing public domestic rural extension and
development channels.
Its potential leverage ratio is 1:40 thanks to
the possibility of scaling up the realignment
of domestic public finance flows with climate
objectives (USD 1.5 billion over 20 years).
The project focuses on one of Mexico’s
poorest and most vulnerable regions, the
population of which includes Indigenous
Peoples.
II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2.
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and, if considered
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/B.39/02.
2.1 Project background

=== PAGE 105 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.11
Page 105
3.
The Balsas Basin in Mexico, a region highly vulnerable to climate change, faces
exacerbated challenges as one of the country’s poorest and most marginalized areas. Climate
effects such as increased temperatures, erratic rainfall, and extended dry seasons are reducing
agricultural productivity and degrading natural resources. These changes lead to more frequent
flooding, crop damage and droughts, fu rther reducing crop yields. Local efforts to combat these
issues are hindered by inadequate capacities and institutional frameworks that fail to prioritize
cl imate adaptation in vulnerable areas.
4.
The cross-cutting project (78 per cent adaptation, 22 per cent mitig ation) aims to
increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and to
red uce emissions from production systems in the BALSAS project area by:
(a)
Operationalizi ng the use of climate-change criteria in current institutional modalities for
the prioritization of rural development incentives through a community-centric bottom-
up approach;
(b)
Investing in the climate-sensitive management of ecosystems, and climate-resilient
productive systems and value chains for adaptation and mitigation benefits; and
(c)
Supporting a climate information and risk management system, and the systematic
management of knowledge and lessons learned.
5.
The cost for the proposed project is estimated at USD 90,953,000, of which GCF
in vestment is requested of USD 20,000,000 in loans and USD 19,500,000 in grants.
6.
The project is expected to achieve a mitigation outcome of 3,926,876 tonnes of carbon
2
dioxide equivalent (t CO eq) over 20 years and to increase the climate resilience of 3,309,211
people, including 201,867 (6.5 per cent of the of the population of prioritized watersheds and
In digenous territories) as direct beneficiaries.
7.
GCF rates the programme as category B (medium) for environmental and social
safeguards.
2 .2 Component-by-component analysis
8.
The project is split into three components leading to the following outcomes:
(a)
Outcome 1: Government programmes and community development plans integrate
climate-change criteria for the allocation of support;
(b)
Outcome 2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate
change for the provision of environmental services and increased livelihood resilience;
and
(c)
Outcome 3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the BALSAS project area
receive timely and relevant climate information for climate risk management, disaster
prevention, and knowledge management.
Component 1: Operationalization of a system that prioritizes sites, beneficiaries and interventions
for rural development support in the Balsas Basin using climate-change criteria (Total cost: USD
10,091,726; GCF cost: USD 2,492,891 in loans and USD 6,128,047 in grants)
9.
The outcome of component 1 is that government programmes and community
de velopment plans integrate climate-change criteria for the allocation of support.
10.
The activities of component 1 are:
(a)
Activity 1.1.1. Facilitate the creation of forest management units and regional
committees for the integrated management of the territory;

(b)
Activity 1.1.2. Agree on priority intervention areas at the micro-basin level;
(c)
Activity 1.1.3. Strengthen multisectoral coordination structures, operation rules, and a
mechanism for co-investment within the basins;
(d)
Activity 1.1.4. Strengthen and operationalize criteria for adaptation to climate change in
institutional programme procedures;
(e)
Activity 1.2.1. Create a communication and awareness campaign to disseminate calls for
proposals;
(f)

III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria
16.
The programme scores well against GCF investment criteria. A detailed assessment of
the programme’s fit with GCF investment criteria is provided below.
Scale: Medium to high
3. 1 Impact potential
17.
The impact potential is expected to be medium to high, as the number of direct
beneficiaries total 201,867 (members of agrarian communities, small producers, and
Indigenous groups), including 40 per cent of women. Likewise, a large mitigation impact with
2
3,926,876 Mt CO eq over 20 years will be achieved from the reduced degradation and therefore
en hanced conservation of 324,578 hectares of forests.
18.
Direct beneficiaries of the project are defined as small producers of agrarian
communities from 121 of the most socially and climate vulnerable municipalities in the BALSAS
project area, focusing on Indigenous communities (82 of the 121 municipalities). These direct
beneficiaries are assessed with a focus on those impacted by land use cover change and value
chain investments in component 2, as well as those benefiting from capacity-building and
br igades across all components.
19.
Indirect beneficiaries of the project, totalling 3,107,344 individuals, are defined as
members of the households of the direct beneficiaries, as well as other community members
from the 68 agrarian communities targeted. Indirect beneficiaries were primarily estimated as a
proportion of the inhabitants residing in the prioritized watersheds, where investments in
component 2 are scheduled to occur. Beneficiaries of early warning systems (component 3)
correspond to the population of high-priority municipalities. However, as these municipalities
largely overlap with the priority watersheds, they are not double counted in the total of indirect
be neficiaries, reflecting a conservative approach.
20.
While primarily adaptation focused (78 per cent), the cross-cutting project also includes
a mitigation component (22 per cent). The project will contribute to Mexico’s 2030 national
target of reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 22 per cent by implementing
afforestation, reforestation and forest, farmland, and pasture management (land use, land-use
change and forestry sector); and agricultural soil management activities and programmed soil
burns (agriculture sector).
Scale: High
3. 2 Paradigm shift potential
21.
The programme has the potential to be scaled up by enhancing public finance flows
aligned with climate objectives and establishing a sustainable environment for institutional
coordination, knowledge and data collection, and skill development. It is estimated that the

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.11
Page 108
average of USD 160 million per year in traditional forestry finance provided by CONAFOR –
totalling more than USD 1.54 billion (net present value) over the project’s 20-year lifespan – will
be redirected towards climate change adaptation and mitigation finance to benefit Mexico’s
mo st vulnerable communities.
22.
The project also has high potential for replication across the country through the
operationalization of climate change criteria in the prioritization of rural development
incentives and by strengthening of multisectoral coordination structures to re-align other
sources of national development finance towards climate finance, as mentioned above.
Scale: High
3. 3 Sustainable development potential
23.
The programme contributes to key Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 13
(Climate action), SDG 1 (No poverty); SDG 2 (Zero hunger); SDG 3 (Good health and well-being);
SD G 5 (Gender equality); SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation); and SDG 15 (Life on land).
24.
The major environmental co-benefit is the retention, regulation and release of rainwater
flows within the ecosystems and the landscapes as both prolonged drought and more severe
and heavy rainfalls pose important hazards for local communities and especially for the most
vulnerable rural livelihoods that depend on forest products and agriculture for which a
reg ulated water flow and soil humidity is vital.
25.
Another main environmental co-benefit is to promote the conservation of biodiversity
and forest resources, resulting in increased availability of habitat for wildlife, the preservation
of culturally and aesthetically important areas, and improved ecosystem service supplies, such
as disease regulation.
Scale: Medium to high
3. 4 Needs of the recipient
26.
The population of 15.8 million people living in the BALSAS project area is extremely
vulnerable to climate-change impacts due to considerably higher poverty rates and lower social
development levels compared to the rest of Mexico. Inequities affecting women’s access to
services and assets have worsened under climate-change effects. Additionally, about 55 per cent
of the population, mainly young people, lack economic opportunities and community decision-
making involvement, reducing their resilience to climate change. Moreover, over 9.6 million
Ind igenous People living in poverty in the municipalities are particularly vulnerable.
27.
Though the Government of Mexico continues to invest in the country’s forestry sector,
there are several obstacles preventing the flow of tailored funding for climate change adaptation
and mitigation in the project area, such as not specifically focusing on the most vulnerable
communities and ecosystems, limited access to credit for promoting the inclusion of small
producers, and lack of inclusion of women and youth in ecosystem management and value chain
act ivities.
28.
The project provides financial and technical support to these vulnerable areas, and
incorporates community needs through participatory planning.
Scale: High
3. 5 Country ownership
29.
This project is highlighted as the top priority in Mexico’s pipeline by its national
designated authority, the Mexican Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (Secretaría de Hacienda
y Crédito Público, referred to as HACIENDA), and has been developed in collaboration with
multiple institutions and stakeholders under the lead of CONAFOR and the guidance of the

=== PAGE 109 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.11
Page 109
national designated authority. It is listed as one of the 100 promises of the favourite candidate
in the upcoming presidential elections.
30.
A no-objection letter has been issued by the country.
31.
The project will aim to use and strengthen existing rural extension and development
channels.
Scale: High
3. 6 Efficiency and effectiveness
32.
The estimated total cost of the project is USD 91 million, of which USD 73.5 million will
be used in investment activities in component 2.
33.
The potential leverage ratio is 1:40 through mobilization of USD 1.5 billion over 20 years
if s caled up across Mexico.
34.
The economic and financial analysis shows that this project is economically viable as a
whole. The main sources of quantifiable benefits result from the investments in component 2
and include the increase in productivity, resilience of the productive systems to climate change,
an d mitigation benefits from GHG emission reductions.
35.
The economic results at the aggregate level of the project are estimated based on the
number of agrarian communities and producer groups to be covered by the project. This step
also includes the benefits related to climate-change mitigation through the impact on GHG
emission reductions estimated with the EX-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (EX-ACT) for the different
mo dels.
36.
With various scenarios of social carbon prices and discount rate, the economic internal
rate of return of the project reaches a value between 16.6 per cent and 34.6 per cent and a
positive economic net present value between USD 25 million and USD 216 million.

4. 1 EPrnovjeircto bnamckegnrotauln adn. d social safeguards
37.
The project aims to increase the climate resilience of the most
vulnerable communities and ecosystems and reduce emissions from production systems in the
BALSAS project area by enabling community-level investment in adaptation and mitigation
initiatives, through the climate-focused transformation of rural development incentives for the

38.
The
accredited entity (AE) has assigned an E&S risk category B rating to the project, given that the
project will have potential limited adverse environmental and social impacts that are site
specific and can easily be mitigated. This also takes account of the fact that the project’s
activities are focused on the protection, conservation and restoration of natural resources and
promote models of sustainable use of resources, such as water, forests and their timber and
non-timber products. The project will not be involved in actions and investments for the
development of aquaculture or fishing, either on a medium or large scale, in ecologically
sensitive areas and will not encourage the conversion of wetlands, changes in hydrology or the
introduction of exotic marine species. The Secretariat concurs with the categorization and
confirms that it is within the E&S risk category that the AE is accredited for. The environmental
and social co-benefits include conservation of biodiversity and improved access by the
communities to environmental services. An environmental and social management framework
(ESMF) has been prepared, which indicates the processes and guidelines for the preparation,

40.
The environmental and social risks and impacts under the project are expected to be
of moderate scale and can be readily managed and mitigated through the deployment of
appropriate measures. Given the nature of the interventions, which are mostly related to
capacity-building, environmental restoration, and targeted agricultural/agroforestry activities,
there are no significant or cumulative impacts expected on biodiversity, natural habitats, land or
water contamination, or on labour and working conditions. The ESMF follows the management
procedures for identification, mitigation or compensation for any possible adverse
environmental and social impacts derived from the execution of the BALSAS project. The ESMF
will be used as guidance in examining the risks and impacts of the project and its subprojects
when establishing the details of the exact locations and the potentially affected peoples. The

The activities of the project will mostly be
implemented on the ground by the local inhabitants of the and rural and Indigenous
communities that are present within the locality. It is expected that the labour of migrants or

include occupational health and safety risks, such as heat stress and dehydration due to

hazards (e.g. allergic reactions to plants, pollen and insect bites and contracting vector-borne
diseases). The project will provide occupational health and safety training and promote the use
of safety equipment when conducting the activities. The project will ensure that international
and national labour regulations, including the AE and GCF requirements, will be applied by

42.
The project intends to promote
measures to reduce pollution and increase the sustainable use of resources, given that the
project is focused on sustainable use of forest and water resources. Nevertheless, the activities
could lead to generation of liquid and solid waste as a result of agricultural production and
processing activities (e.g. crop residue, animal manure, agrochemical containers, harvesting and
processing waste, packaging material) that could pollute land and water resources. Land and
water contamination may also occur from the use of fuels and lubricants in machinery or the
use of pesticides and/or agrochemicals. To mitigate these impacts, the project will support the
implementation of agroecological best practices such as composting, mulching, recycling
packaging materials and ensuring that the machinery is properly maintained and serviced.
Farmers will also be trained on the safe use and handling of agrochemicals. The project will also
promote measures to reduce water use, such as drop irrigation and rainwater harvesting which
could enhance infiltration, reduce soil erosion, improve water quality and minimize silting of

the risk of the presence of organized crime in the project region, which may affect project

al abuse and sexual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding.
48.
The project is considered by the Secretariat to potentially face high SEAH risks and impacts,
given the existing prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV) and social conflicts linked to non-
compensatory water extraction practices, water access difficulties and a lack of employment
opportunities in protected natural areas, which disproportionally affect rural women, youth and
Indigenous Peoples in the Balsas Basin. As the project aims to strengthen local watershed
governance and the participation of women, youth and Indigenous Peoples at the sub-basin
level, the AE will trigger ESS standards related to labour and working conditions and
community health and safety to prevent and mitigate GBV and SEAH-related risks and impacts
related to the project. Moreover, the AE will further identify and assess specific GBV and SEAH
risks as part of the subproject due diligence and through a social risk assessment to be
conducted at the community level. Detailed SEAH risk assessments and relevant mitigation
measures will be required as part of the subproject-specific environmental and social
management plans. The AE will ensure the safety and security of women’s groups in the target
communities, and will find age-, gender- and culturally-appropriate ways to facilitate the
participation of women in the project. The AE will also enhance community awareness,
capacities and strengths of project beneficiary communities, implementing partners and
government staff in preventing and reducing the GBV and SEAH risks, and proactively map the
existence of and gaps in services for survivors/affected people. In the case of an SEAH incident,
the project will follow the reporting and response protocols already established by the AE that
include specific SEAH-related procedures to provide essential services and redress to

=== PAGE 112 ===

The ESMF describes the existing institutional level

complaints that may be received by the project. It also describes the AE’s grievance mechanism

Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Focal Point will be available for assistance at any
stage, including before a claim has been made.
4. 2 Gender policy
52.
The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan, and therefore complies with the
GCF Gender Policy. The project is designed to stimulate climate investments that contribute to
reducing gender gaps and promote equality through climate-change mitigation and adaptation
measures. Based on the findings of the gender analysis, recommendations were formulated into
a gender action plan (GAP) to ensure women’s participation in project activities and benefits by
defining participation targets and capacity-building for gender equality for the different groups
of project stakeholders. The GAP is elaborated on the basis of the project’s expected results and
outputs framework and defines activities, indicators and targets for women’s participation
alo ng with an estimated budget.
53.
The GAP aims to strengthen women’s participation in decision-making, promote greater
gender equity in institutions, meaningfully include women as beneficiaries of investment plans,
build capacity through training on gender and inclusion, and use selection criteria for payment
of environmental services projects that favour women’s participation. Additionally, the project
will build women’s capacity to strengthen the systems of the farming communities, be trained in
production practices and technologies adapted to climate change and variability, and benefit
from the financial transfers made by the project. Climate-resilient sustainable forest
management activities will open opportunities for women to participate in value chains, such as
those linked to the sustainable use of and value addition to timber and non-timber forest
products while also allowing the exchange of experiences and good practices implemented by
women in the project.

GCF/B.39/02/Add.11
Page 113
54.
The AE is strongly encouraged to both measure and promote women-led and managed
.
proposals and projects related to integrated land and water resources management This would
further align with the aim of the project to strengthen women’s leadership and further minimize
the risk of women being marginalized.
44.3.3.1. O Rveisraklsl p rogramme assessment (medium risk)
55.
This funding proposal is a cross-cutting project (78 per cent adaptation and 22 per cent
mitigation) to strengthen the climate resilience of vulnerable communities and reduce
emissions in the Balsas Basin in Mexico. To achieve its objectives, the project aims to implement
a climate-sensitive ecosystem management system, a climate-resilient productive system, and a
climate information and risk management system in the country. GCF is considering a loan of
USD 20 million and a grant of USD 19.5 million together with co-financing of USD 51.5 million in
loan and grant from IFAD and the Government of Mexico. The co-financing ratio is 1:1.3 and
a 4. c 3 c .2 o . untAs cfocrr e5d7i tpeedr ceennttit oyf/ teoxtealc ufitnianngc einngt.i ty capability to execute the current programme
(low risk)
56.
IFAD is an international AE with over 40 years of development work in global
agriculture and food security. To date, IFAD has provided over USD 23 billion in grants and low-
interest rate loans to fund livelihoods in fragile and developing countries. The project’s EEs are
HACIENDA, NAFIN, and CONAFOR. As the focal point for international climate finance in the
country, Ministry of Finance and Public Credit will sign the subsidiary loan agreement with
IFAD, and NAFIN will act as the intermediary and fiduciary of the project loans. Responsibility
for project execution will rest with CONAFOR, which also manages the country’s National Forest
Fund. Since its creation in 2001, CONAFOR has developed a track record of working on
internationally funded forestry projects and programmes. CONAFOR has worked with IFAD for
over 15 years, including on the implementation of loans and grants for forest areas and
v 4. u 3. l 3 n . eraPblreo pjeocptu-slapteiocnifsi.c execution risks (medium risk)
57.
In addition to the three EEs mentioned above (HACIENDA, NAFIN and CONAFOR),
decisions are overseen by a steering committee that reports to political decision makers who
approve the working plan and select implementation partners. CONAFOR is one of five steering
committee members. Under the steering committee are two layers of technical coordination
teams and units which provide technical advice. These are followed by regional committees and
local co-implementing partners, and at the bottom are the grass-roots organizations,
communities and forestry enterprises. Given the multilayered and cross-institutional
arrangement, operational risk and potential delays due to poor coordination is a concern. This
risk is partly mitigated by the project’s initiative to strengthen intersectoral coordination and
regular engagement with stakeholders. The project will also benefit from the support of the
Office for Coordination of Advisers and HACIENDA. The funding proposal presents low credit
risk given the investment grade BBB rating of Mexico as the debtor. Although a portion of the
funding will be denominated in United States dollars to fund local currency generating activities,
foreign currency risk is likewise limited because this will be backstopped by the government’s
investment grade rating. With regard to the risk of local currency devaluation after
disbursement affecting the project budget, this will be managed by planning currency
conversion as close as possible to expected disbursement.

4.3.4. Project viability and concessionality
58.
The proposed project will develop plans to integrate climate change into government
programmes to improve capacity and strengthen coordination. Other components will enhance
sustainable forest landscapes while enabling timely climate information availability to manage
climate risk. All these initiatives are well within the expertise of the AE and EEs given their track
record in this space, and the project is expected to be viable. The requested participation from
GCF will represent approximately 43 per cent of total programme size in a project funded partly
by loans and partly by grants. The level of concessionality of one of the loans appears high in
view of an economy as creditworthy and large as that of Mexico. However, it is intended to be
blended to make a forest payment of environmental services scheme viable over a 40-year
period. The lower concessionality loan is intended for revenue-generating activities that are
m 4.3 o .5 r . e ecCoonmompilciaanllyc ev iraibskle ( bmute dthiuatm w roiuslkd) n ot have been funded otherwise.
59.
Mexico faces a number of challenges in terms of internal conflict and social unrest. This
is largely due to the presence of drug cartels and criminal gang violence, including in the
targeted region. The proposal by the AE (IFAD) does address this issue and sets out measures to
address and mitigate any consequences or impacts. The AE has also created its programme to
assure appropriate financial integrity practices, such as preventing money-laundering, terrorist
financing and prohibited practices. For any proposed disbursement of funds or commodities to
beneficiaries directly, IFAD has established clear guidelines as to eligibility and measures to
monitor and prevent abuse. Having considered the context in which the proposal will be

4. 4 Fiduciary
61.
CONAFOR’s International Affairs and Financial Promotion Unit in conjunction with the
project coordination unit will handle the project’s financial management. This management
str ucture adheres to the legal framework governing public administration in Mexico.
62.
NAFIN will be the administrator for the project’s financing. Responsibilities include
following up on audits, managing disbursements and overseeing loan disbursement processes.
The GCF funds will be channelled through the AE (IFAD) via NAFIN and the Treasury to
CO NAFOR.
63.
There will be two methods for the disbursements of funds: reimbursement for GCF Loan
